## Run 9: 30 min Hard Data analytics, Data Analyst (strong candidate)

**Overall 70/100.** Communication 7, technical 7, confidence 7, problem-solving 7, relevance 7. AI cost ₹9.25.

**What went well:** Clear stakeholder communication; Strong metric discipline; Thoughtful SQL diagnosis; Sound experiment design

**To improve:** Ground examples in outcomes; Make reporting assumptions explicit; Protect incomplete comparisons

**Answers to work on:** Provide a verified finding (What was a specific finding from the weekly risk report, and how would you explain its significance to the product manager?); Resolve month-end eligibility (Could you write SQL that carries forward each eligible loan’s last known balance when it has no snapshot in a month, and calculates the change across consecutive calendar months?); Guard partial balance changes (Could you finish the query and explain how it distinguishes a product with no eligible loans from one with incomplete balance data?); Connect lift to uncertainty (It lifted on-time repayments by 6%. What does that figure mean, and how would you assess whether the result is reliable?)

<details><summary>Transcript</summary>

**Interviewer (Q1)** [0:00]: Welcome! We'll explore your lending analytics experience and how you would apply it to retail banking. Tell me about the collections dashboard you built and how the 40 agents used it.

**Candidate** [1:05]: At the lending startup, agents were spending too much time piecing together borrower status from different reports, so I built a Power BI collections dashboard to give them one clear view. I worked with the collections team to understand their daily workflow, then used SQL to bring together repayment status, days past due, and contact history. I organized the dashboard so agents could quickly prioritize accounts and see the next useful action, rather than just review totals. We rolled it out to 40 agents, who used it in their day-to-day collections work. It gave the team a shared, consistent view of the portfolio and made it easier to focus outreach on the accounts that needed attention.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [1:05]: Can you give a specific example of how an agent used the dashboard to decide which account to contact and what action to take?

**Candidate** [2:04]: For example, an agent could filter to accounts that were newly past due, then sort by balance and check the borrower’s recent contact history. If the dashboard showed a high-balance account with no recent outreach, the agent could prioritize it and make a direct call; if there had already been a recent reminder, they could avoid duplicating it and move to another account. The dashboard brought those signals together in one place, so agents could make that choice quickly. I don’t have a verified individual account story or a measured time-savings figure, but it supported that kind of prioritization across the 40-agent team.

**Interviewer (Q2)** [2:04]: Moving on to your risk reporting: How would you explain the purpose and main findings of your weekly risk report to a non-technical product manager?

**Candidate** [3:13]: I’d explain that the weekly risk report was an early-warning view of whether borrowers were becoming more likely to miss payments, so product managers could spot changes and decide where to investigate. I built the underlying SQL to summarize repayment behavior and risk indicators in a consistent way from week to week. I’d avoid leading with query details and instead focus on what changed, which customer groups were affected, and whether the movement looked meaningful. In my experience, the most concrete result I can point to is that a reminder-message A/B test improved on-time repayments by 6%. I’d clearly separate that finding from the weekly report itself, then explain what it could mean for product decisions and what further analysis would be needed before acting.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [3:13]: What was a specific finding from the weekly risk report, and how would you explain its significance to the product manager?

**Candidate** [4:19]: I don’t want to invent a specific finding—the resume confirms I wrote the SQL for the weekly risk report, but doesn’t specify its results. I’d explain a verified finding in plain language by saying which borrower group or repayment measure changed, how much it changed, and whether the trend was large enough to warrant attention. For instance, if the report showed rising missed payments among borrowers in a particular segment, I’d frame that as a signal to investigate whether onboarding, repayment timing, or communications were contributing—not as proof of a cause. I’d then recommend a focused follow-up analysis. Separately, one confirmed result from my work was that an A/B test of reminder messages lifted on-time repayments by 6%.

**Interviewer (Q3)** [4:19]: Let's move to a risk metric. A risk manager asks for the delinquency rate. What definition would you propose, and what would you clarify before publishing it?

**Candidate** [5:29]: I’d propose a clearly scoped measure: the percentage of accounts that are delinquent on a given reporting date—accounts at least 30 days past due divided by accounts with an active repayment obligation on that date. I’d also show the count and, where useful, a balance-weighted rate, since those answer different questions. Before publishing, I’d confirm the aging threshold, whether the denominator is accounts or borrowers, how we treat closed, charged-off, restructured, or disputed accounts, and whether delinquency is based on scheduled or actual payment dates. I’d align the snapshot date and time zone, check data completeness and duplicates, and make the definition consistent with the manager’s existing reporting. Finally, I’d label the metric and document the filters so it can be reproduced and compared week to week.

**Interviewer (Q4)** [5:29]: Let's return to the collections dashboard. Describe a decision you made while building it when the requirements were unclear.

**Candidate** [6:35]: One unclear requirement was what agents meant by “accounts to prioritize.” They wanted a useful worklist, but there wasn’t a shared definition of priority. I spoke with the collections team about how they chose their next calls and what information they checked, then shaped the dashboard around practical signals such as days past due, balance, repayment status, and recent contact history. I kept the view focused on helping agents identify accounts needing attention, rather than adding every available metric. I also treated prioritization as a way to organize their work, not as an automated decision about how a borrower should be treated. That gave the 40 agents a consistent view they could use in their daily collections workflow.

**Interviewer (Q5)** [6:35]: Let's move on to SQL. Given loan and payment tables, how would you find each customer's most recent payment, including customers who have never paid?

**Candidate** [7:47]: I’d start from the customer or loan table so customers with no payments stay in the result, then left join payments and rank each customer’s payments newest first. For example:

```sql
WITH ranked_payments AS (
  SELECT
    loan_id,
    payment_date,
    amount,
    ROW_NUMBER() OVER (
      PARTITION BY loan_id
      ORDER BY payment_date DESC
    ) AS rn
  FROM payments
)
SELECT
  l.customer_id,
  l.loan_id,
  p.payment_date AS most_recent_payment_date,
  p.amount AS most_recent_payment_amount
FROM loans l
LEFT JOIN ranked_payments p
  ON l.loan_id = p.loan_id
 AND p.rn = 1;
```

The left join leaves the payment fields null when a loan has never had a payment. I’d clarify whether “each customer” means one latest payment across all their loans or the latest payment per loan. If it’s per customer, I’d partition by customer ID instead, joining customer IDs into the ranking step.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [7:47]: Could you write the query for one most recent payment per customer across all their loans, including customers who have never paid?

**Candidate** [8:54]: Assuming there’s a `customers` table and payments link to loans, I’d rank payments across all loans for each customer, then left join the latest one back to every customer:

```sql
WITH ranked_payments AS (
  SELECT
    l.customer_id,
    p.payment_date,
    p.amount,
    ROW_NUMBER() OVER (
      PARTITION BY l.customer_id
      ORDER BY p.payment_date DESC, p.payment_id DESC
    ) AS rn
  FROM loans l
  JOIN payments p
    ON p.loan_id = l.loan_id
)
SELECT
  c.customer_id,
  rp.payment_date AS most_recent_payment_date,
  rp.amount AS most_recent_payment_amount
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN ranked_payments rp
  ON rp.customer_id = c.customer_id
 AND rp.rn = 1;
```

The customer-based partition gives one latest payment across all of each customer’s loans. The `payment_id` tie-breaker makes the result deterministic when payment dates match, and the left join retains customers who have never paid.

**Interviewer (Q6)** [8:54]: Let’s discuss your reminder-message test. It lifted on-time repayments by 6%. What does that figure mean, and how would you assess whether the result is reliable?

**Candidate** [10:07]: The 6% figure means the test group had a higher on-time repayment outcome than the comparison group, but I’d be precise about whether that was a relative lift or a six-percentage-point increase. I wouldn’t assume the distinction without checking the original analysis. To assess reliability, I’d confirm customers were randomly assigned, the groups were comparable, and the test ran long enough to cover the relevant repayment cycle. I’d check the sample size and confidence interval, and verify we used a consistent definition of on-time repayment with no important data-quality issues. I’d also look for differences across customer segments and make sure the result wasn’t driven by one unusual week. If the estimate were statistically and practically meaningful, I’d recommend rollout with continued monitoring rather than treating the 6% as a guaranteed impact for every group.

**Interviewer (Q7)** [10:07]: Let’s consider a reporting discrepancy. A product team reports that repayments improved while the risk team sees worsening delinquency. How would you investigate the apparent contradiction?

**Candidate** [11:21]: First, I’d check whether we’re comparing the same population and time period. “Repayments improved” might refer to payment volume or on-time rates for one segment, while delinquency could be a portfolio-wide snapshot. I’d align the date windows, cohort definitions, loan status filters, and metric denominators, and confirm both teams use the same source data and as-of date. Then I’d break the results down by origination cohort, product, and days past due to see whether improvement in one group is masking deterioration elsewhere. I’d also check for timing effects: repayments may have risen this week while delinquency reflects balances accumulated over a longer period. Finally, I’d validate the underlying joins and data freshness, reconcile a sample of records, and present the two measures with their definitions before concluding whether there’s a real trend or a reporting mismatch.

**Interviewer (Q8)** [11:21]: Now, thinking about a different audience: How would you redesign your collections dashboard for retail banking managers without losing what made it useful for agents?

**Candidate** [12:35]: I’d preserve the agent-level worklist and clear account signals, but add a manager view focused on team performance and portfolio trends. Managers could see delinquency and repayment patterns by branch, product, or team, alongside workload and outreach outcomes, with filters that let them drill down to the same account details agents use. I’d make sure the summary metrics have explicit definitions and a consistent reporting date, so managers can compare teams fairly. I’d also be careful with access controls and avoid exposing customer details to users who don’t need them. I’d validate the redesign with both audiences: agents should still be able to prioritize their daily work quickly, while managers should be able to spot trends and ask useful follow-up questions. That keeps the original dashboard’s practical value while adapting the level of detail to management decisions.

**Interviewer (Q9)** [12:35]: Let's move to another SQL problem. Given daily loan balance snapshots, write SQL to calculate month-end outstanding balance and month-over-month change by product, accounting for loans with missing month-end snapshots.

**Candidate** [13:57]: I’d first clarify whether a missing month-end snapshot means “use the latest available balance that month” or “carry forward the last known balance.” Assuming it means latest available snapshot within each month, and each loan has one row per snapshot date:

```sql
WITH monthly_loan AS (
  SELECT
    product,
    loan_id,
    DATE_TRUNC('month', snapshot_date) AS month,
    outstanding_balance,
    ROW_NUMBER() OVER (
      PARTITION BY loan_id, DATE_TRUNC('month', snapshot_date)
      ORDER BY snapshot_date DESC
    ) AS rn
  FROM loan_balance_snapshots
),
monthly_product AS (
  SELECT
    product,
    month,
    SUM(outstanding_balance) AS month_end_balance
  FROM monthly_loan
  WHERE rn = 1
  GROUP BY product, month
)
SELECT
  product,
  month,
  month_end_balance,
  month_end_balance
    - LAG(month_end_balance) OVER (
        PARTITION BY product ORDER BY month
      ) AS mom_change
FROM monthly_product
ORDER BY product, month;
```

This selects each loan’s last available snapshot in each month, then sums by product. I’d also check whether missing snapshots should instead be carried forward, and how to handle loans that close or change products mid-month.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [13:57]: How would you handle loans with no snapshot anywhere in a month, and ensure the change compares consecutive calendar months?

**Candidate** [15:11]: I’d build a calendar-month spine for each product, then left join the monthly balances to it. For a month with no snapshot for a loan, I’d carry forward that loan’s last known balance only if the business definition treats snapshots as state; otherwise I’d leave it missing and flag the coverage gap. Once product totals have one row per calendar month, `LAG` compares consecutive months. For example, I’d use a calendar table from the earliest to latest reporting month crossed with the product list, left join the monthly totals, and calculate `month_end_balance - LAG(month_end_balance)`. I’d avoid using `LAG` directly on sparse results, because that would compare to the prior month with data, not necessarily the prior calendar month. I’d also distinguish a true zero balance from missing data so reporting doesn’t imply a balance that wasn’t observed.

**Interviewer (Q10)** [15:11]: Let’s return to the reminder-message test. An executive wants to launch it to all customers, but the sample was small. How would you communicate your recommendation?

**Candidate** [16:21]: I’d say the result is promising—the test showed a 6% lift in on-time repayments—but a small sample means we shouldn’t treat that estimate as conclusive or assume it will hold across all customers. I’d explain the uncertainty in plain language, including the confidence interval and the range of customer groups represented, after verifying those details in the analysis. My recommendation would be a staged rollout: expand the test to a larger, representative group, keep a holdout for comparison, and monitor repayment outcomes as well as opt-outs or complaints. If the effect remains meaningful and there are no adverse signals, we can broaden deployment. That lets us capture potential value while limiting the risk of scaling a result that may be noisy or apply only to the original sample.

**Interviewer (Q11)** [16:21]: Let’s look at another reporting issue. A dashboard’s delinquency rate differs from the weekly SQL risk report. How would you trace the discrepancy and prevent it from recurring?

**Candidate** [17:30]: I’d first pin down the exact metric in both places: reporting date, delinquency threshold, numerator, denominator, population filters, and whether the measure is account-, customer-, or balance-weighted. Then I’d compare the underlying records for the same date, starting with counts at each transformation step to find where they diverge. I’d check refresh timing, time zones, joins that might duplicate or drop loans, and treatment of closed or charged-off accounts. Once we identify the cause, I’d document the agreed definition and reconcile both outputs against a trusted source. To prevent recurrence, I’d centralize the metric logic in a shared, tested data model where possible, add automated checks for key totals and freshness, and make the dashboard and weekly report use the same definition and reporting date.

**Interviewer (Q12)** [17:30]: Let’s discuss the SQL report itself. Your SQL risk report is slow and occasionally duplicates loan balances after joining transactions. How would you diagnose and fix both problems without changing the intended results?

**Candidate** [18:43]: I’d first establish a baseline: capture the current report’s row counts, key totals, and a sample of loan-level results, then inspect the query plan and table sizes. For the duplicates, I’d check the grain of each table and whether a loan-to-many-transactions join is multiplying snapshot balances. I’d aggregate transactions to the intended loan and reporting-period grain before joining, or use an `EXISTS` check if the report only needs to know whether a transaction occurred. I’d verify the fix against the baseline, including edge cases, so the intended definitions and totals don’t change. For performance, I’d reduce scanned data with date filters, select only needed columns, review join keys and indexes or partition pruning, and avoid repeated transformations. I’d rerun timing and reconciliation checks, then document the grain and tests so future changes don’t reintroduce duplication.

**Interviewer (Q13)** [18:43]: Let’s move to experiment design. How would you test whether a new repayment reminder reduces delinquency, while accounting for customer-level randomization, multiple loans, and potential spillover?

**Candidate** [20:00]: I’d define the outcome and follow-up window first—for example, whether a customer becomes 30 or more days past due within a fixed period after an eligible payment due date. Because customers can hold multiple loans, I’d randomize at the customer level and assign all of that customer’s eligible loans to the same arm, avoiding cross-arm exposure. I’d predefine how to combine loan outcomes, such as a customer-level indicator for any eligible loan becoming delinquent, and use customer-level analysis or standard errors clustered by customer. I’d stratify randomization on key factors like baseline delinquency and loan count to improve balance. To limit spillover, I’d ensure messaging is tied to the customer’s assigned treatment across channels and check for shared accounts or household effects if relevant. I’d also set a sample-size and duration plan, track delivery and opt-outs, and analyze on an intention-to-treat basis.

**Interviewer (Q14)** [20:00]: Now consider a stakeholder challenge. A senior risk leader questions your finding because it conflicts with their experience and asks you to remove an unfavorable segment from the dashboard. How would you respond?

**Candidate** [21:09]: I’d take the challenge seriously and ask what experience or data they think the finding may be missing. Then I’d recheck the segment’s definition, source data, sample size, and relevant time window, and share the analysis and assumptions transparently. If we find a genuine data or interpretation issue, I’d correct it and document the change. If the finding holds, I wouldn’t remove an unfavorable segment just to make the dashboard more comfortable; that could obscure a real risk. I’d explain the evidence and offer context, such as showing the segment’s size, uncertainty, or trend separately. If there’s a legitimate reason to exclude it for a particular decision, I’d make that filter explicit and preserve the full-population view so users can see what’s included and why.

**Interviewer (Q15)** [21:09]: Let’s consider one final scenario. Product and risk disagree on the definition of on-time repayment, and a launch depends on a dashboard due tomorrow. How would you resolve the disagreement and communicate the trade-offs?

**Candidate** [22:24]: I’d bring both teams together quickly and ask what decision the dashboard needs to support, then compare their proposed definitions against the payment schedule and available data. For example, one definition might count payment on the due date, while another allows a grace period; those can produce different rates and influence a launch decision. I’d check which definition is already used in official reporting and whether timestamps and late-posted payments can be measured reliably. If we can’t reach agreement by tomorrow, I’d publish a clearly labeled provisional metric using the established definition, include the alternative rate if feasible, and state the assumptions and limitations. I’d avoid presenting either as definitive until the owners agree. After the deadline, I’d document the canonical definition, align the underlying SQL and dashboard logic, and add a validation check so future reporting stays consistent.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [22:24]: Who would make the final decision if product and risk still disagree by the launch deadline, and what would you do if no decision is reached?

**Candidate** [23:36]: The metric owners should recommend the definition, but if product and risk still disagree, I’d escalate the decision to the accountable launch owner—typically the product or risk executive responsible for accepting that launch’s measurement risk. I’d provide a concise comparison of both definitions, their impact on the reported result, and the decision each would support. If no decision is reached by the deadline, I wouldn’t present an ambiguous metric as settled or choose a definition on their behalf. I’d label the dashboard provisional, show both rates with their definitions if the data supports it, and clearly flag that the launch decision needs an owner’s sign-off. If the metric is essential to launch readiness, I’d recommend delaying the decision until the definition is agreed; otherwise, the launch owner can explicitly accept the uncertainty.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [23:36]: Returning to the month-end balance problem, could you write SQL that carries forward each eligible loan’s last known balance when it has no snapshot in a month, and calculates the change across consecutive calendar months?

**Candidate** [25:21]: Assuming a loan’s balance should carry forward after its first snapshot, and eligibility is determined by a loan eligibility table, I’d create a calendar spine for each eligible loan and month. Then I’d select the latest snapshot on or before each month-end, sum balances by product, and use `LAG` over the complete calendar sequence.

```sql
WITH months AS (
  SELECT month_start
  FROM calendar_months
  WHERE month_start BETWEEN :start_month AND :end_month
),
eligible_loan_months AS (
  SELECT
    e.loan_id,
    e.product,
    m.month_start,
    m.month_start + INTERVAL '1 month' - INTERVAL '1 day' AS month_end
  FROM eligible_loans e
  CROSS JOIN months m
  WHERE e.eligible_from <= m.month_start + INTERVAL '1 month' - INTERVAL '1 day'
    AND (e.eligible_to IS NULL OR e.eligible_to >= m.month_start)
),
loan_month_end AS (
  SELECT
    elm.loan_id,
    elm.product,
    elm.month_start,
    (
      SELECT s.outstanding_balance
      FROM loan_balance_snapshots s
      WHERE s.loan_id = elm.loan_id
        AND s.snapshot_date <= elm.month_end
      ORDER BY s.snapshot_date DESC
      LIMIT 1
    ) AS balance
  FROM eligible_loan_months elm
),
product_month AS (
  SELECT product, month_start, SUM(balance) AS month_end_balance
  FROM loan_month_end
  GROUP BY product, month_start
)
SELECT
  product,
  month_start,
  month_end_balance,
  month_end_balance - LAG(month_end_balance) OVER (
    PARTITION BY product ORDER BY month_start
  ) AS mom_change
FROM product_month
ORDER BY product, month_start;
```

A loan with no snapshot yet remains `NULL`, rather than being treated as zero.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [25:21]: How would you adjust this query to ensure every product has a row for every calendar month, and to flag product totals when eligible loans have no prior snapshot?

**Candidate** [26:58]: I’d build the month spine across all products, then left join the aggregated loan balances. I’d also count eligible loans with no snapshot on or before month-end; that makes the total visibly incomplete rather than silently treating unknown balances as zero.

```sql
WITH months AS (
  SELECT month_start,
         month_start + INTERVAL '1 month' - INTERVAL '1 day' AS month_end
  FROM calendar_months
  WHERE month_start BETWEEN :start_month AND :end_month
),
product_months AS (
  SELECT p.product, m.month_start, m.month_end
  FROM products p
  CROSS JOIN months m
),
loan_months AS (
  SELECT
    e.product, e.loan_id, pm.month_start, pm.month_end,
    (SELECT s.outstanding_balance
     FROM loan_balance_snapshots s
     WHERE s.loan_id = e.loan_id
       AND s.snapshot_date <= pm.month_end
     ORDER BY s.snapshot_date DESC
     LIMIT 1) AS balance
  FROM eligible_loans e
  JOIN product_months pm ON pm.product = e.product
  WHERE e.eligible_from <= pm.month_end
    AND (e.eligible_to IS NULL OR e.eligible_to >= pm.month_start)
),
totals AS (
  SELECT product, month_start,
         SUM(balance) AS known_balance,
         COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE balance IS NULL) AS loans_without_prior_snapshot
  FROM loan_months
  GROUP BY product, month_start
)
SELECT pm.product, pm.month_start,
       t.known_balance AS month_end_balance,
       COALESCE(t.loans_without_prior_snapshot, 0) AS loans_without_prior_snapshot,
       (COALESCE(t.loans_without_prior_snapshot, 0) > 0) AS total_incomplete,
       t.known_balance - LAG(t.known_balance) OVER (
         PARTITION BY pm.product ORDER BY pm.month_start

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [26:58]: Could you finish the query and explain how it distinguishes a product with no eligible loans from one with incomplete balance data?

**Candidate** [28:20]: ```sql
SELECT
  pm.product,
  pm.month_start,
  t.known_balance AS month_end_balance,
  COALESCE(t.eligible_loan_count, 0) AS eligible_loan_count,
  COALESCE(t.loans_without_prior_snapshot, 0) AS loans_without_prior_snapshot,
  CASE
    WHEN COALESCE(t.eligible_loan_count, 0) = 0 THEN 'no eligible loans'
    WHEN t.loans_without_prior_snapshot > 0 THEN 'incomplete balance data'
    ELSE 'complete'
  END AS data_status,
  t.known_balance - LAG(t.known_balance) OVER (
    PARTITION BY pm.product ORDER BY pm.month_start
  ) AS mom_change
FROM product_months pm
LEFT JOIN totals t
  ON t.product = pm.product
 AND t.month_start = pm.month_start
ORDER BY pm.product, pm.month_start;
```

In the `totals` CTE, count eligible loans separately from loans whose carried-forward balance is null:

```sql
COUNT(*) AS eligible_loan_count,
COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE balance IS NULL) AS loans_without_prior_snapshot
```

A product-month with no eligible loans has a null joined total and a coalesced eligible count of zero. A product-month with eligible loans but missing prior snapshots has a positive eligible count and a positive missing-snapshot count. The status makes those cases explicit; the known balance is only a partial total in the incomplete case.

**Interviewer** [28:20]: Thank you for your time and for sharing your experience. Your interview report is being prepared.

</details>

## Run 11: 45 min Medium Technical interview, Backend Software Engineer (average candidate)

**Overall 56/100.** Communication 6, technical 6, confidence 4, problem-solving 6, relevance 6. AI cost ₹28.73.

**What went well:** Strong checkout correctness; Thoughtful retry handling; Practical production judgment; Clear incident communication

**To improve:** Substantiate personal experience; Make mechanisms concrete; Close consistency gaps

**Answers to work on:** Missing latency evidence (Can you give a specific example of a caching or query change, including the evidence that led to it and its measured impact?); Unclear incident ownership (Can you describe a specific production issue you personally investigated, including the signals you used, your actions, and the outcome?); Ownership not established (What were your main responsibilities for the online grocery order service, and which production changes did you personally own?); Cache consistency gap (During the gap between the Postgres commit and the cache update, how would you prevent a stale reader from repopulating the cache?); Show the SQL (How would you find all orders placed by a customer in the past 30 days?); Specify the response (What HTTP status and response body would you use, and why?); Compare technical tradeoffs (What technical requirements might lead you to choose Java over Go, or vice versa?); Detail concurrent reservation (How would you safely use a transaction when creating an order and reserving inventory in the same Postgres database?)

<details><summary>Transcript</summary>

**Interviewer (Q1)** [0:00]: Welcome! We'll discuss your backend experience and work through some practical checkout engineering scenarios. What were your main responsibilities for the online grocery order service?

**Candidate** [0:39]: I was mainly responsible for the backend order flow, from creating an order through its status updates. I worked on maintaining the service, fixing production issues, and coordinating with other teams when changes affected checkout. I also helped move parts of the system into separate services. Basically, I wanted the order flow to stay reliable and straightforward for customers.

**Interviewer (Q2)** [0:39]: Let's move on to API design. What makes an HTTP API suitable for creating a checkout order?

**Candidate** [1:17]: I think it should be clear about what information the client needs to send, and it should validate that information before creating the order. It should return a useful response with the order’s status and a way to check it later. It also needs to handle retries safely, so a repeated request doesn’t accidentally create another order.

**Interviewer (Q3)** [1:17]: Let's move on to SQL. How would you find all orders placed by a customer in the past 30 days?

**Candidate** [1:53]: I’d query the orders table using the customer’s ID and a condition on the order creation time, keeping only rows from the past 30 days. I’d return the fields the caller needs and sort the results newest first. Basically, I’d also check that the query uses an index rather than scanning every order.

**Interviewer (Q4)** [1:53]: Turning to your experience, which part best prepares you to own a checkout service in production?

**Candidate** [2:31]: I think my on-call experience best prepares me for that. I’ve had to respond when backend issues affected the order flow, work out what was happening, and coordinate fixes with other teams. That gave me a practical sense of how important clear ownership and communication are in production. I’d bring that same steady approach to a checkout service.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [2:31]: Could you describe a specific on-call incident, including what you did and the outcome?

**Candidate** [3:13]: I remember an incident where order processing slowed down, and customers were waiting longer for confirmation. I checked the service and database behavior, then worked with the team to identify a query that was taking too long. We made a focused fix and watched the service afterward to make sure it recovered. It reminded me to communicate clearly while investigating, not just focus on the code.

**Interviewer (Q5)** [3:13]: Let's discuss checkout failures. How would you explain the difference between a timeout and an application error to a teammate investigating a failed checkout?

**Candidate** [3:51]: A timeout means the caller didn’t get a response in time, so it may be unclear whether the checkout actually completed. An application error means the service returned a response saying it couldn’t complete the request. I’d be careful not to treat a timeout as proof that no order was created, and check the order state before retrying.

**Interviewer (Q6)** [3:51]: Let's consider implementation choices. How would you decide whether to implement a new checkout component in Java or Go?

**Candidate** [4:28]: I’d first look at what the existing checkout services use and whether the team can support that language well. I’d also consider whether the component needs to fit into existing libraries and deployment practices. Basically, I wouldn’t choose based only on personal preference; I’d aim for consistency and something the team can operate and maintain comfortably.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [4:28]: What technical requirements might lead you to choose Java over Go, or vice versa?

**Candidate** [5:07]: I’d consider the requirements around integrating with existing systems and the libraries the component needs. If checkout already relies on Java services and shared tools, Java might fit more naturally. If a small component needs to be simple to build and run, Go could be a good fit. I think the team’s ability to support the choice matters too.

**Interviewer (Q7)** [5:07]: Let's move on to API responses. How would you design a response for a checkout request that fails because an item is out of stock?

**Candidate** [5:45]: I’d return a clear error response saying the item is out of stock, along with enough information for the client to show the customer what happened. I’d avoid suggesting the checkout succeeded, and I’d make sure the response is consistent with other checkout errors. If possible, the client should be able to update the cart and try again.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [5:45]: What HTTP status and response body would you use, and why?

**Candidate** [6:22]: I’d use a conflict-style status because the request was understandable, but the stock changed before checkout could complete. The response body would include a stable error code and a simple message explaining that the item is unavailable. That lets the client handle the case clearly without depending on the exact wording of the message.

**Interviewer (Q8)** [6:22]: Let's turn to production monitoring. What information would you check first if checkout latency suddenly increased after a deployment?

**Candidate** [7:02]: I’d first check whether the increase began right after the deployment and whether it affects all checkout requests or only part of the flow. Then I’d look at the service’s errors and resource usage, along with any downstream dependencies that may have slowed down. Basically, I’d compare the current behavior with the previous version and use that to guide the investigation.

**Interviewer (Q9)** [7:02]: Let's discuss Postgres performance. What index would you consider for a query that retrieves a customer's recent orders, and how would you verify that it helps?

**Candidate** [7:43]: I’d consider an index that starts with the customer identifier and includes the order creation time, since the query filters by customer and sorts by date. I’d check the query plan before and after adding it, and test with realistic data to see whether Postgres uses the index and the query behaves better. I’d also make sure the index fits the actual query.

**Interviewer (Q10)** [7:43]: Let's discuss your order service. How did you identify the caching and query changes that reduced its p95 latency?

**Candidate** [8:23]: I looked at where time was being spent in the order flow, then focused on repeated reads and database queries that seemed unnecessarily expensive. I tested changes against the real request patterns and checked whether they improved the slow requests without changing behavior. Basically, it was a process of measuring, making a small change, and verifying it before rolling it out.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [8:23]: Can you give a specific example of a caching or query change, including the evidence that led to it and its measured impact?

**Candidate** [9:03]: One example was caching data that was read repeatedly during order processing, so the service didn’t need to make the same database lookup every time. I chose that after noticing those reads were common in the request flow. I checked the latency after the change and saw the slow requests improve, while also watching for stale data or incorrect order behavior.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [9:03]: What data did you cache, and what were the p95 latency figures before and after the change?

**Candidate** [9:39]: I cached frequently read order-related data that didn’t need to be fetched from the database on every request. The evidence was that repeated lookups were adding time to the order flow. I don’t recall the exact before-and-after figures for that individual change, but I checked the overall latency and correctness after rollout.

**Interviewer (Q11)** [9:39]: Let's consider retries. A client retries a checkout request after a timeout. How would you prevent the retry from creating a duplicate order?

**Candidate** [10:21]: I’d have the client send a unique idempotency key with the checkout request. The service would store the result associated with that key, so if the same request is retried, it can return the existing order rather than create another one. I’d also make sure the key is scoped to the customer and that the behavior is consistent if the first request is still being processed.

**Interviewer (Q12)** [10:21]: Let's consider a product tradeoff. How would you explain the tradeoffs of caching product prices during checkout to a product manager?

**Candidate** [11:01]: Caching prices can make checkout faster and reduce repeated work, but it risks using a price that has changed since it was cached. I’d explain that the right choice depends on how current the price must be at the moment of purchase. For checkout, I’d generally verify the price before confirming the order, and clearly handle any change for the customer.

**Interviewer (Q13)** [11:01]: Let's move on to database transactions. How would you safely use a transaction when creating an order and reserving inventory in the same Postgres database?

**Candidate** [11:42]: I’d put the order creation and inventory reservation in one database transaction, so they either both succeed or both get rolled back. I’d check that enough stock is available as part of the reservation, and only confirm the order if that succeeds. Basically, I’d keep the transaction focused and handle conflicts by returning a clear checkout failure rather than leaving a partial order.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [11:42]: How would you prevent two concurrent transactions from reserving the same remaining stock?

**Candidate** [12:19]: I’d make the stock check and update happen atomically in the database, rather than reading the quantity and updating it separately. That way, concurrent requests can’t both rely on the same old value. If a reservation can’t be made, the transaction should fail cleanly and the checkout should report that the item is unavailable.

**Interviewer (Q14)** [12:19]: Let's consider an on-call incident. What would you do if you suspected a recent release was causing checkout failures but had incomplete evidence?

**Candidate** [12:58]: I’d first check the customer impact and compare the failures with the release timing, while looking for other likely causes. If the impact seemed serious, I’d coordinate with the team and consider rolling back the release rather than waiting for perfect evidence. I’d keep communication clear, then continue investigating and verify checkout behavior after the immediate issue is addressed.

**Interviewer (Q15)** [12:58]: Let's discuss the monolith split. What boundaries did you use when splitting it into three services, and how would that experience inform a checkout service design?

**Candidate** [13:35]: I’d split around areas with distinct responsibilities and clear ownership, rather than just dividing code by size. In the grocery system, that helped make changes easier to isolate. For checkout, I’d keep the order flow and its responsibilities clear, and avoid creating service boundaries that make a simple customer action depend on too many separate steps.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [13:35]: What were the three service boundaries in your grocery system, and why were they chosen?

**Candidate** [14:15]: The split was around order handling, catalog information, and customer accounts. Those areas had different responsibilities and different patterns of change, so separating them made ownership clearer. I think the main reason was to reduce coupling while keeping the customer-facing order flow understandable. I’d still be careful to make each boundary useful rather than splitting services just for the sake of it.

**Interviewer (Q16)** [14:15]: Let's turn to database performance. How would you investigate a Postgres query that is fast in testing but slow under peak checkout traffic?

**Candidate** [14:55]: I’d first compare the production query and data conditions with the test setup, because peak traffic can change the amount of data and contention. I’d look at the query plan and database load during the slowdown, then check whether the query is waiting on resources or doing more work than expected. I’d try to reproduce the pattern safely before changing it.

**Interviewer (Q17)** [14:55]: Let's turn to incident communication. How would you communicate the status of a checkout outage to engineers and business stakeholders while the cause is still unknown?

**Candidate** [15:33]: I’d share what we know, what customers are experiencing, and what the team is doing next, while being clear that the cause isn’t confirmed yet. I’d give engineers the useful technical signals and keep business stakeholders focused on impact and mitigation. I think regular updates matter, and I’d avoid guessing or making promises before we have evidence.

**Interviewer (Q18)** [15:33]: Let's discuss payment failures. How would you handle a payment provider timing out after receiving a charge request, when you don't know whether the charge succeeded?

**Candidate** [16:09]: I’d treat the payment result as unknown rather than assume the charge failed. I’d avoid sending a new charge blindly, and use the provider’s request identifier or status lookup to find out what happened. Meanwhile, I’d keep the checkout state clear and avoid confirming the order until the payment outcome is known.

**Interviewer (Q19)** [16:09]: Let's discuss downstream notifications. How would you use Kafka to notify downstream services that an order was created without losing the event if the database write succeeds?

**Candidate** [16:46]: I’d use a transactional outbox: write the order and an event record in the same database transaction, then have a separate process publish that record to Kafka. That way, the event isn’t lost if the database write succeeds but Kafka is temporarily unavailable. Consumers should also handle duplicate events safely, since publishing may be retried.

**Interviewer (Q20)** [16:46]: Let's consider an inventory design. A teammate proposes caching inventory counts in Redis to speed up checkout. Would you approve the design as stated?

**Candidate** [17:28]: I wouldn’t approve it as stated without understanding how the cache would stay consistent with the database. Inventory can change quickly, so a stale count could allow checkout to promise stock that isn’t available. I’d ask how reservations are made and verified, and make sure the database remains authoritative before confirming an order. Redis might still help, but not as the final source of truth.

**Interviewer (Q21)** [17:28]: Let's discuss operational practices. Which monitoring and on-call practices from your previous role would you bring to a checkout team, and why?

**Candidate** [18:07]: I’d bring clear ownership, useful alerts, and a habit of reviewing incidents after the immediate issue is resolved. Alerts should point to customer impact rather than create noise, and the team should know who is responding and how to share updates. Basically, I think monitoring is most useful when it helps people act quickly and learn from recurring problems.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [18:07]: Can you describe a specific alert or on-call practice you used in your previous role and how it helped?

**Candidate** [18:44]: One practice I’d bring is having a clear on-call lead who coordinates investigation and communication during an incident. That keeps engineers focused on diagnosing the issue while someone tracks customer impact and shares updates. I think it helped the team stay organized, especially when several people needed to work on different parts of the problem.

**Interviewer (Q22)** [18:44]: Let's consider a cross-service incident. Checkout errors rise intermittently across multiple services, but each service's local logs look healthy. How would you investigate and coordinate the response?

**Candidate** [19:25]: I’d first look for a shared dependency or a common point in the checkout flow, since each service’s local logs may not show the whole request. I’d compare timing and request identifiers across services, and ask the relevant owners to check their signals. I’d keep one person coordinating updates, focus on customer impact, and avoid making changes until we have a clearer picture.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [19:25]: What specific cross-service signals would you use to trace an intermittent failed checkout and identify where it broke down?

**Candidate** [20:05]: I’d use a request or trace identifier that follows the checkout across services, then compare each step’s timing and outcome to see where the request stopped progressing. I’d also look at service-level error rates and dependency timeouts around the same period. Basically, the goal is to connect the pieces into one request history rather than relying on each service’s logs alone.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [20:05]: Returning to the order service latency work, what specific query did you change, and what evidence showed it was contributing to p95 latency?

**Candidate** [20:45]: I don’t recall the exact query text, but it was a database lookup in the order flow that was taking longer than expected. We noticed it while reviewing slow-request behavior and query performance, then adjusted the query and checked latency afterward. I’d want to look back at the original investigation notes before naming the exact query or claiming a specific impact.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [20:45]: How would you use traces and Postgres query metrics to determine whether a lookup is contributing to checkout p95 latency?

**Candidate** [21:26]: I’d start with traces from slow checkout requests and see how much time is spent in the database lookup compared with the rest of the request. Then I’d compare the query’s latency and frequency in Postgres metrics during the same period, and check whether it’s slower under load. If both point to the lookup, I’d investigate its plan and test a change carefully.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [21:26]: How would you use EXPLAIN ANALYZE to identify why that lookup is slow under load?

**Candidate** [22:04]: I’d run EXPLAIN ANALYZE on the query with representative data and look for steps that take unexpectedly long or process many rows. I’d compare that with the estimated plan and check whether the filters and indexes match the query. Since load can affect behavior, I’d also review production metrics rather than assume a local run explains everything.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [22:04]: If EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows a large difference between estimated and actual row counts, how would you investigate it?

**Candidate** [22:44]: I’d check whether the table statistics are up to date, since stale statistics can lead Postgres to estimate row counts poorly. I’d also look at the filters and data distribution to see whether the query is more selective or uneven than expected. Then I’d refresh or improve the statistics if needed and compare the plan again, while checking the production behavior too.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [22:44]: How would you make an inventory reservation atomic in Postgres? Please give an example SQL statement.

**Candidate** [23:23]: I’d use a conditional update so the database checks availability and reserves stock as one operation. For example: `UPDATE inventory SET quantity = quantity - 1 WHERE product_id = $1 AND quantity > 0 RETURNING quantity;` If it returns a row, the reservation succeeded; if not, there wasn’t stock available. I’d run this as part of the order transaction.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [23:23]: How would you extend that approach to reserve multiple items in one checkout without leaving a partial reservation or causing deadlocks?

**Candidate** [24:01]: I’d handle all the item reservations in one transaction and roll the whole transaction back if any item can’t be reserved, so there’s no partial reservation. To reduce deadlock risk, I’d process items in a consistent order across requests. I’d also make sure failures are handled cleanly and that the transaction doesn’t stay open longer than necessary.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [24:01]: How would you handle a deadlock if one still occurs during checkout?

**Candidate** [24:39]: I’d let the database abort one transaction, then make sure the application treats that as a retryable failure rather than reporting a successful checkout. I’d retry carefully with a small limit and some delay, and ensure the operation is safe to repeat. I’d also log enough context to see whether a particular reservation pattern is causing repeated deadlocks.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [24:39]: How would you implement the idempotency key so that two concurrent checkout requests with the same key cannot create two orders?

**Candidate** [25:20]: I’d store the idempotency key with a unique constraint in the database, scoped to the customer. When a request arrives, the service would try to claim that key as part of the order transaction. If another request already claimed it, the second request would read and return the existing result rather than create another order. The unique constraint handles the race between concurrent requests.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [25:20]: How would you handle the same idempotency key being reused with a different checkout request body?

**Candidate** [25:57]: I’d store a fingerprint of the original request alongside the idempotency key. When the key is reused, I’d compare the new request with that stored fingerprint. If they differ, I’d return a clear client error rather than returning the result for a different checkout request. That keeps retries safe without letting one key represent multiple orders.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [25:57]: How would you handle an idempotency record whose original checkout is still processing when a concurrent retry arrives?

**Candidate** [26:37]: I’d have the retry check the existing record and recognize that the original request is still in progress. It should not start another order. I’d return a response indicating that processing is underway, and let the client check again later using the same key. The record needs a clear state so the service can distinguish in-progress work from a completed result.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [26:37]: How would you recover an in-progress idempotency record if the worker processing the original checkout crashes?

**Candidate** [27:17]: I’d make the in-progress record recoverable rather than leaving it stuck forever. I’d store enough state to tell whether the work can safely be resumed, and have a recovery process identify records that appear abandoned. Before retrying, it should check whether an order or payment was already created, so recovery doesn’t duplicate side effects. I’d keep the recovery behavior conservative and observable.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [27:17]: How would a recovery process distinguish an abandoned checkout from one that is still being processed, while preventing two workers from resuming it simultaneously?

**Candidate** [27:57]: I’d track a lease or heartbeat on the idempotency record, so a worker periodically shows that it is still active. A recovery worker could look for records whose lease has expired, then claim one atomically before resuming it. That prevents two workers from taking the same record at once. I’d also verify the checkout state before repeating any external side effects.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [27:57]: What would happen if the original worker resumed after its lease expired and another worker had claimed the checkout?

**Candidate** [28:34]: I’d make the claim include a changing ownership token or version, and require that token when the worker updates the checkout record. If the original worker resumes with an old token, its write should be rejected. I’d also make any external side effects idempotent where possible, so both workers can’t accidentally complete the same checkout.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [28:34]: If the original worker sends a payment request after losing its lease, how would you prevent a duplicate charge?

**Candidate** [29:13]: I’d use a stable payment idempotency key for that checkout and send the same key on every attempt. Then the payment provider can recognize retries as the same charge instead of creating another one. I’d also check the payment status before deciding how to continue, because the request might have succeeded even if the worker didn’t receive a response.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [29:13]: What would you do if the payment provider did not support idempotency keys?

**Candidate** [29:52]: I’d avoid blindly retrying a payment request whose outcome is unknown. I’d first check whether the provider offers a way to look up the original request or reconcile charges. If there’s no reliable way to confirm the outcome, I’d keep the checkout in a pending state and involve the provider’s support or an operational review before attempting another charge.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [29:52]: Returning to your latency work, what specific tools or signals did you use to identify repeated database reads as a caching opportunity?

**Candidate** [30:33]: I used request-level latency and database observations to see where the order flow was spending time, and looked for reads that happened repeatedly for the same data. I’d compare those patterns with the request path before deciding caching was appropriate. I don’t recall the exact tool names, but I’d verify the behavior with traces or query metrics and check the effect after rollout.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [30:33]: How would you decide which order-related data is safe to cache, and how would you handle invalidation when it changes?

**Candidate** [31:12]: I’d cache data that is read often and doesn’t need to reflect every change immediately, while keeping important checkout decisions based on current database state. I’d choose a clear expiration period and invalidate the cache when the underlying data changes. Basically, I’d start conservatively, monitor for stale results, and avoid using cached data as the final authority for order correctness.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [31:12]: Can you describe a specific race between a database update and cache invalidation, and how you would handle it?

**Candidate** [31:52]: A race can happen if a cache is invalidated before the database update commits, then another request reads the old database value and puts it back into the cache. I’d generally update the database first, then invalidate the cache after commit. For data where that still isn’t enough, I’d use a short expiry and ensure checkout verifies important values against the database.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [31:52]: What could go wrong if a reader fetches the old database value before the update commits but writes it to the cache after invalidation? How would you address that race?

**Candidate** [32:33]: That reader could repopulate the cache with stale data after the invalidation, so later requests may keep seeing the old value until it expires. I’d address it with a version or generation check so an older read can’t overwrite newer state, or use a cache-aside approach with careful invalidation and a short expiry. For checkout decisions, I’d still verify critical data against the database.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [32:33]: How would you implement the version check so a stale reader cannot repopulate the cache after an update?

**Candidate** [33:15]: I’d keep a version for the data and include it with the cached value. When a reader is about to write its result, it should only do so if the version is still current. The update would advance that version when it commits. If the reader has an older version, it skips the cache write. I’d make the check atomic so the comparison and write can’t race.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [33:15]: Where would you store the version, and how would you coordinate updating it with the Postgres commit so the cache check remains reliable?

**Candidate** [33:54]: I’d store the authoritative version in Postgres with the data, and update it in the same transaction as the data change. After commit, the application can update or invalidate the cache using that version. I’d be careful about the gap between the database commit and the cache operation, and use a reliable mechanism to retry cache updates if they fail.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [33:54]: During the gap between the Postgres commit and the cache update, how would you prevent a stale reader from repopulating the cache?

**Candidate** [34:35]: I’d avoid relying on a separate cache update alone for correctness. One approach is to have readers check the database version before writing a value into the cache, and only write if it still matches. The commit can still race with that check, so I’d use an atomic cache-side version check and keep checkout’s critical decisions grounded in Postgres rather than trusting cached data.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [34:35]: Can you walk through the exact sequence of operations for the database update and cache write, including how the cache-side version is advanced?

**Candidate** [35:16]: I’d update the data and its version together in Postgres, then commit. After that, I’d advance the cache version and invalidate the cached value. A reader would fetch the database value and version, then only populate the cache if its version is not older than the cache’s current version. I’d make that comparison and write atomic, and retry cache updates if they fail.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [35:16]: If a reader writes the old value to the cache after the database commit but before the cache version advances, what happens, and how would your design handle it?

**Candidate** [35:59]: In that gap, the stale value could still be written if the cache hasn’t learned about the newer version yet. I’d avoid treating that cache entry as authoritative, and have readers validate its version against the database or a reliable version marker before using it. For checkout decisions, I’d keep Postgres as the source of truth, and use the cache mainly to improve reads where brief staleness is acceptable.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [35:59]: What specific data was cached in your grocery order service, and how was its freshness requirement determined?

**Candidate** [36:36]: I don’t recall the exact field or freshness policy well enough to name it confidently. The general approach was to look for data that was read repeatedly in the order flow, then check whether it could tolerate being briefly stale. Anything that affected the correctness of confirming an order needed validation against the current database state.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [36:36]: Can you describe a specific production issue you personally investigated, including the signals you used, your actions, and the outcome?

**Candidate** [37:18]: I investigated a slowdown in order processing that affected customer confirmations. I compared the timing of the issue with service and database behavior, then worked with the team to narrow it to a slow database lookup. We adjusted the query and monitored the service afterward to make sure it recovered. I don’t recall the exact query or metrics, but the experience reinforced checking evidence before changing things.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [37:18]: For that slowdown, what actions did you personally take to narrow the issue to the database lookup?

**Candidate** [37:58]: I checked where the slowdown showed up in the order flow and compared that with database behavior around the same time. I then reviewed the lookup with the team and helped confirm it was taking longer than expected. After we changed it, I watched the service to see whether the customer-facing delay improved. I don’t recall the exact tools or query details.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [37:58]: What specific change did you or the team make to the slow lookup?

**Candidate** [38:38]: I don’t recall the exact query change well enough to describe it confidently. It was a focused adjustment to the lookup after we reviewed its behavior, rather than a broader redesign. I’d want to check the change history before claiming whether it involved the query itself or how it accessed the data, and I’d verify the result against the original production signals.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [38:38]: How would you distinguish a slow query from database connection-pool contention during an order-processing slowdown?

**Candidate** [39:20]: I’d compare time spent waiting to get a database connection with time spent executing the query. If requests are queued before they reach Postgres, that points toward pool contention; if the query itself takes longer once it starts, I’d investigate the query and database load. I’d also check whether connections are being held too long or not released properly, using the service’s database metrics and traces.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [39:20]: What would you check to determine whether increasing the connection-pool size would help or make the slowdown worse?

**Candidate** [39:59]: I’d check how often requests are waiting for connections, how busy Postgres is, and whether existing connections are being used efficiently. If the database is already overloaded, a larger pool could add more pressure rather than help. I’d look at the pattern during the slowdown and test any change carefully, instead of increasing the pool just because requests are waiting.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [39:59]: What specific pool and Postgres metrics would you compare before deciding whether to increase the pool size?

**Candidate** [40:39]: I’d compare the application’s active, idle, and waiting connections, along with how long requests wait to borrow a connection. On Postgres, I’d check active sessions, query duration, CPU and I/O pressure, and whether sessions are blocked. If the pool is saturated while the database has capacity, a small adjustment might help; if Postgres is already busy, it could make things worse.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [40:39]: How would you safely test a small connection-pool increase in production, and what signals would prompt you to roll it back?

**Candidate** [41:17]: I’d make the change gradually, ideally on a limited portion of traffic, and compare connection wait time and checkout latency with the baseline. I’d also watch Postgres load, query times, and errors to see whether the extra connections are adding pressure. If database performance or checkout reliability worsened, I’d roll back and investigate other causes before trying again.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [41:17]: How would you account for the total number of Postgres connections across all service instances before increasing the pool size?

**Candidate** [41:58]: I’d calculate the total possible connections from each service’s pool across all running instances, including deployment overlap and other applications sharing Postgres. I’d compare that with the database’s connection limit and leave room for administrative access. I’d also check actual peak usage, since configured pool capacity isn’t always the same as active connections, and avoid increasing it without understanding the overall connection budget.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [41:58]: What were your main responsibilities for the online grocery order service, and which production changes did you personally own?

**Candidate** [42:39]: I was responsible for maintaining the order flow, handling production issues, and working with other teams on changes that affected checkout. I also contributed to separating parts of the system into services and improving how it handled requests. I don’t want to overstate which specific changes I personally owned without checking the details, but I was involved in the service’s ongoing reliability and development.

**Interviewer** [42:39]: Thank you for your time. Your report is being prepared.

</details>

## Run 12: 45 min Hard Managerial interview, Technical Project Manager (strong candidate)

**Overall 52/100.** Communication 6, technical 5, confidence 4, problem-solving 6, relevance 5. AI cost ₹19.47.

**What went well:** Clear stakeholder communication; Sound scenario judgment; Transparent about evidence

**To improve:** Substantiate personal contributions; Support estimates with evidence; Be precise about claims

**Answers to work on:** No personal decision example (Thinking beyond the migration, can you describe a specific delivery decision you personally made on any project, including the options, evidence, and outcome?); Readiness claim unsubstantiated (You mentioned clear testing and rollback plans before each migration cutover. What can you personally recall doing to confirm those plans were ready?); Risk ownership missing (Can you describe a specific risk decision you made during the migration, including who owned the mitigation and how you tracked it?); Estimation method unclear (How did you and the engineering team estimate the five-month payments migration, and how did you check the estimate as work progressed?); No disagreement example (Tell me about a time you challenged an engineering lead's estimate or scope recommendation. How did you resolve the disagreement?); Dependency example missing (Can you describe one specific dependency, its owner, and how you resolved or tracked it during the migration?); Controls not identified (What technical and operational controls enabled the migration to finish without downtime?); Savings basis unverified (How did you measure the reported 18% reduction in transaction costs, and what might make that comparison misleading?)

<details><summary>Transcript</summary>

**Interviewer (Q1)** [0:00]: Welcome, and thanks for joining us today. We'll discuss your experience delivering payments products and explore how you approach challenging project decisions. What aspects of your payments platform migration best prepare you to manage delivery for our payments products?

**Candidate** [1:05]: The migration prepared me because it combined technical complexity, commercial impact, and a hard reliability constraint. We moved our payments platform to a new provider over five months with eight engineers, while keeping transactions available throughout. I aligned engineering and business stakeholders on scope and milestones, managed dependencies and release readiness, and made sure testing and rollback plans were clear before each cutover. We completed the migration with no downtime and reduced transaction costs by 18%. That experience taught me to treat payments delivery as more than a technical launch: you have to protect customer trust, control operational risk, and track the business outcome at the same time. I’d bring that same disciplined approach to your products.

**Interviewer (Q2)** [1:05]: Turning to your distributed teams, how do you keep your Bengaluru and Berlin Scrum teams aligned on priorities and the release calendar?

**Candidate** [2:14]: I make the priorities visible and create a reliable cadence across both teams. For example, I run the release calendar for our Bengaluru and Berlin Scrum teams, with a shared view of milestones, dependencies, and decision owners so neither team is working from a different version of the plan. We use refinement and planning to agree on outcomes and acceptance criteria, then keep handoffs explicit in the work items and document decisions for the team that isn’t online. I also use a short overlap window for cross-team blockers rather than adding meetings everywhere. When priorities change, I confirm the impact with both teams and stakeholders, update the calendar, and communicate what moves and why. That helps us stay coordinated while giving each team room to execute.

**Interviewer (Q3)** [2:14]: Turning to your card platform experience, how did gathering requirements and managing UAT help you lead technical delivery?

**Candidate** [3:18]: At the retail bank, I was a business analyst on the card platform, gathering requirements from business stakeholders and supporting UAT. That experience taught me to translate business needs into testable outcomes, rather than passing engineering a list of ambiguous requests. I worked through edge cases with stakeholders, clarified acceptance criteria, and used UAT feedback to identify gaps before release. It helps me lead technical delivery because I can connect the “why” behind a feature to what teams need to build and how we’ll know it works. In my later payments migration, I used the same discipline around scope, readiness, and validation, which helped the team deliver without downtime while reducing transaction costs by 18%.

What I did do on the retail bank card platform was gather requirements and support UAT, using stakeholder feedback to clarify whether the delivered behavior met the intended business outcome. That experience shaped how I now plan delivery: I define acceptance criteria early, make edge cases explicit, and leave room in the release plan to assess UAT findings. If a finding affects a critical user journey or creates operational risk, I’d work with the product and engineering leads to agree whether it requires a fix before release, a revised criterion, or a documented follow-up.

**Interviewer (Q4)** [4:23]: Moving on to the migration plan, how did you and the engineering team estimate the five-month payments migration, and how did you check the estimate as work progressed?

**Candidate** [5:33]: We estimated the five-month migration by breaking it into workstreams and milestones with the eight engineers, then reviewing dependencies, testing, and release readiness alongside the build work. I kept the plan tied to observable progress rather than treating the initial estimate as fixed: we reviewed milestones in team planning, surfaced blockers and dependency changes, and compared actual progress with the release calendar. If something threatened a milestone, I worked with engineering to understand the impact and adjust sequencing or escalate a decision early. The specific estimation technique and checkpoint metrics aren’t details I can substantiate from my resume, so I wouldn’t claim a particular method. What I can say is that we completed the migration in five months, with no downtime, and reduced transaction costs by 18%.

**Interviewer (Q5)** [5:33]: Turning to migration risks, what were the most consequential risks in your provider migration, and how did you decide which ones needed active mitigation?

**Candidate** [6:42]: The most consequential risk was disrupting payment processing during the provider cutover, because even a short interruption could affect customers and revenue. For the cutover risk, that meant making release readiness and continuity central to the plan, with engineering validating the migration before launch. I don’t want to imply specific controls that aren’t on my resume, but the outcome was a migration completed with no downtime and transaction costs reduced by 18%. That experience reinforced that high-impact risks need clear owners and active attention throughout delivery.

In a similar project, I’d make the risk decision explicit: document the impact and likelihood, assign one accountable owner, agree on a mitigation and trigger for escalation, then review it in the delivery cadence until it’s closed. That approach helps teams act early without overstating what happened in this particular migration.

**Interviewer (Q6)** [7:49]: Moving on to working with engineering leads, tell me about a time you challenged an engineering lead's estimate or scope recommendation. How did you resolve the disagreement?

I’d then update the plan and communicate the rationale so the team can execute against one agreed direction.

**Interviewer (Q10)** [13:22]: Turning to the migration’s commercial outcome, how did you measure the reported 18% reduction in transaction costs, and what might make that comparison misleading?
