## Run 15: 60 min Hard System design, Backend Software Engineer (average candidate)

**Overall 60/100.** Communication 6, technical 6, confidence 5, problem-solving 6, relevance 7. AI cost ₹43.57.

**What went well:** Handled payment uncertainty; Reasoned about migration risks; Explained operational priorities; Addressed reliability patterns

**To improve:** Make assumptions sooner; Add implementation detail; Substantiate experience; Define complete measurements

**Answers to work on:** Calculate an initial estimate (Could you choose reasonable assumptions and calculate an illustrative peak requests-per-second estimate?); Propose measurable targets (What specific availability and latency targets would you propose, and how would you measure them?); Specify the API contract (What would the request and response look like, including how you’d handle validation errors and a retry with the same idempotency key?); Quantify the cache change (Can you describe one specific caching or query change and the measurements that showed how much it contributed to the improvement?); Detail the cutover protocol (How would you move existing orders and ongoing updates to the new service, then cut over authority without missing or duplicating writes?); Connect trace context (How would you trace one checkout attempt across API calls, database writes, and Kafka events while protecting sensitive customer data?)

<details><summary>Transcript</summary>

**Interviewer (Q1)** [0:00]: Welcome! We’ll explore how you design and operate reliable checkout systems, drawing on your experience with order services and production ownership. What were the boundaries of the three services you created from the grocery monolith, and what checkout-related responsibilities did each own?

**Candidate** [0:45]: I think we split the system around the main business responsibilities. One service handled the checkout and order flow, another managed product and availability information, and the third dealt with payment and order updates. The checkout service coordinated the steps and kept track of the order’s status, while each other service owned its own data and rules. We tried to keep the boundaries simple, though there were still some dependencies between them.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [0:45]: Could you clarify which service owned payment status and order updates, and how those responsibilities differed from the checkout service’s ownership of order status?

**Candidate** [1:27]: Basically, the payment service owned the payment outcome, such as whether a charge succeeded or failed, and the updates service handled sending order changes to other parts of the system. The checkout service owned the customer-facing order lifecycle and used those results to move the order forward. I think the exact boundaries were fairly simple, but I’d need to check the implementation to be precise.

**Interviewer (Q2)** [1:27]: Moving on to checkout design: For a retail checkout service, what core operations and constraints would you clarify before proposing a design?

**Candidate** [2:10]: I’d first clarify what the checkout needs to support, such as creating an order, confirming payment, and handling a failure or retry. I’d also ask which system is authoritative for product availability and pricing, and what customers should see if one of those systems is temporarily unavailable. Basically, I’d want to understand the expected behavior and reliability needs before choosing how the services communicate or store their data.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [2:10]: What scale, consistency, and business constraints would you clarify before designing it?

**Candidate** [2:53]: I’d clarify expected traffic patterns, especially whether checkout has sudden peaks, and how quickly the system needs to respond. For consistency, I’d ask whether prices and inventory must be confirmed at checkout or whether a short delay is acceptable. I’d also clarify business rules around duplicate orders, payment retries, and when an order is considered complete, since those affect what the customer is promised and how failures are handled.

**Interviewer (Q3)** [2:53]: Moving on to the API: How would you design an API for creating an order from a customer's cart?

**Candidate** [3:39]: I’d make an endpoint that accepts the cart reference and an idempotency key, then have the server verify prices and availability rather than trusting values from the client. It would return an order identifier and a clear status, such as pending or confirmed. If checkout is still processing, the client could check the order later. I’d keep the response simple and avoid treating a successful request as proof that payment or fulfillment is complete.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [3:39]: What would the request and response look like, including how you’d handle validation errors and a retry with the same idempotency key?

**Candidate** [4:22]: I’d have the request include the cart reference and an idempotency key, with customer details supplied through the authenticated session. On success, I’d return the order identifier and its current status. If validation fails, I’d return a clear error explaining which part of the cart needs attention, without creating an order. If the same key is retried, I’d return the original result rather than create a second order.

**Interviewer (Q4)** [4:22]: Now let’s consider storage: What data would you store in Postgres for an order, its items, and its payment status, and how would you relate them?

**Candidate** [5:04]: I’d store an order record with its customer, status, and totals, then keep each item in a separate table linked to that order. I’d also store payment status and a reference to the payment provider, so the order can be checked against the payment result. I’d preserve the item price used at checkout rather than relying on the current catalog price, since that can change later.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [5:04]: How would you structure the payment data and its relationship to the order, especially if there are multiple payment attempts?

**Candidate** [5:45]: I’d keep payment attempts in a separate table, with each attempt linked to the order, so retries don’t overwrite earlier outcomes. Each record would hold the provider reference, status, and basic timestamps. The order could point to the attempt currently being used, while retaining the history for support and reconciliation. I’d be careful not to store sensitive card details in the order database.

**Interviewer (Q5)** [5:45]: Moving on to operations: A checkout endpoint’s p95 latency rises suddenly; how would you begin investigating?

**Candidate** [6:32]: I’d first check whether the increase affects all requests or only a particular path, and compare it with recent deployments or changes. Then I’d look at the service’s logs and dashboards for errors, resource pressure, and slow dependencies such as the database or payment provider. I think the main goal at first is to narrow down where the time is being spent before making changes, while keeping an eye on whether customers are unable to complete checkout.

**Interviewer (Q6)** [6:32]: Moving on to capacity planning: Your grocery service handled 40,000 orders a day; how would you estimate peak checkout traffic and capacity for a new retailer?

**Candidate** [7:18]: I’d start by looking at the retailer’s traffic patterns and when promotions or other events create peaks, rather than assuming orders are evenly spread across the day. I’d estimate how many customers may reach checkout at once, then test the service under that kind of load and watch its dependencies as well. I’d also leave room for unexpected bursts and use gradual scaling so capacity can respond without overloading the database or payment provider.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [7:18]: Could you walk through a numerical estimate from daily orders to peak requests per second, including your assumptions about retries and headroom?

**Candidate** [8:02]: I’d need the retailer’s traffic profile and checkout flow to make a useful estimate, since daily orders alone don’t show how concentrated the peak is. I’d estimate the share of orders occurring in the busiest period, account for extra requests from retries and status checks, then add headroom for bursts. I’d validate that estimate with a load test and production metrics, and check that downstream services can handle the same peak.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [8:02]: Using 40,000 orders per day, could you choose reasonable assumptions and calculate an illustrative peak requests-per-second estimate?

**Candidate** [8:49]: I’d use 40,000 orders a day as a baseline, but I wouldn’t treat that as the request count because each checkout can involve several calls. For an illustration, I’d assume traffic is concentrated during a busy shopping period, then include retries and status checks before adding headroom. I can’t give a meaningful peak requests-per-second figure without choosing a specific peak window and call pattern, so I’d make those assumptions explicit and validate them with load testing.

**Interviewer (Q7)** [8:49]: Moving on to service targets: How would you define an availability and latency target for checkout, and what trade-offs would you discuss with product stakeholders?

**Candidate** [9:34]: I’d start by asking product what customers experience when checkout is slow or unavailable, and which parts of the flow matter most. Then I’d propose targets for successful checkout requests and response time, based on current performance and business expectations. I’d discuss that stronger safeguards or extra checks can sometimes add delay, while relaxing them may affect correctness. I think the target should reflect customer impact and be monitored consistently in production.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [9:34]: What specific availability and latency targets would you propose, and how would you measure them?

**Candidate** [10:19]: I’d propose a clear availability target for the checkout endpoint and a latency target for the customer-facing response, then review them with product and operations before committing. I’d measure availability from successful requests against eligible requests, and latency with percentiles rather than averages. I’d also separate failures caused by our service from expected business rejections, and track the full checkout flow so a fast response that leaves an order stuck doesn’t look healthy.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [10:19]: Could you propose specific numerical availability and latency targets, along with the time window you’d measure them over?

**Candidate** [11:00]: I’d propose measuring checkout availability and latency over a monthly window, with separate targets for successful request handling and customer-facing response time. I’d choose the exact thresholds after reviewing current performance, customer expectations, and downstream limits, rather than inventing numbers that may not be achievable or useful. I’d also monitor shorter windows for incidents, since a monthly average can hide a bad period.

**Interviewer (Q8)** [11:00]: Moving on to retries: How would you make an order-creation API safe when a client retries after a timeout?

**Candidate** [11:46]: I’d require an idempotency key for each order-creation attempt and store it with the result. If the client times out and retries with the same key, the service can return the existing order rather than create another one. I’d make sure the key is tied to the customer and request, and that concurrent retries don’t create duplicates. The client should reuse the key for that attempt, not generate a new one after every timeout.

**Interviewer (Q9)** [11:46]: Now let’s look at query performance: What indexes would you consider for fetching a customer’s recent orders, and how would you verify that they help?

**Candidate** [12:31]: I’d consider an index on the customer identifier and order creation time, since the query would filter by customer and sort newest first. I’d check the actual query plan and compare its behavior with realistic data before and after adding the index. I’d also watch write performance and storage, because indexes add overhead. The index should match the query we actually run, rather than being added just because those fields seem important.

**Interviewer (Q10)** [12:31]: Moving on to your latency work: You reduced p95 latency from 900 ms to 220 ms; how did you determine which caching and query changes mattered most?

**Candidate** [13:17]: I started by looking at traces and query timings to see where requests were spending time, rather than changing several things at once. Then I focused on repeated reads and slow database queries, testing changes and checking whether the same request paths improved. I think it was important to compare before and after under similar traffic, and to watch for stale data or extra load elsewhere. That helped us understand which changes were useful.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [13:17]: Can you describe one specific caching or query change and the measurements that showed how much it contributed to the improvement?

**Candidate** [14:00]: One specific change was caching frequently read product information so checkout didn’t need to fetch it from the database on every request. We compared the relevant request traces and database activity before and after the change, while watching for stale values. I don’t remember the exact contribution as a separate figure, since the overall improvement came from several changes, but it helped reduce repeated work on that path.

**Interviewer (Q11)** [14:00]: Moving on to the end-to-end flow: Design a checkout flow that coordinates pricing, inventory, payment, and order creation across services.

**Candidate** [14:47]: I’d have checkout validate the cart and get current prices, then ask inventory to reserve the items before attempting payment. If payment succeeds, checkout creates or confirms the order and releases the reservation into the normal fulfillment flow. If payment fails, it cancels the reservation and tells the customer what happened. I’d keep a clear status for each step so the system can recover if a service times out, rather than assuming every call completes successfully.

**Interviewer (Q12)** [14:47]: Moving on to inventory: How would you prevent two customers from purchasing the last available item at the same time?

**Candidate** [15:29]: I’d have the inventory service own the decision and reserve stock atomically, rather than letting checkout read a quantity and make the decision itself. If two requests arrive together, only one should successfully reserve the last item; the other gets an out-of-stock response. The reservation should expire or be released if checkout doesn’t complete, so stock isn’t held indefinitely. I’d also make reservation requests safe to retry.

**Interviewer (Q13)** [15:29]: Moving on to API evolution: How would you version a checkout API when a new pricing model changes the response contract?

**Candidate** [16:13]: I’d try to add the new pricing information in a backward-compatible way first, so existing clients can continue working. If the response meaning or required fields change substantially, I’d introduce a new API version and let clients migrate over time. I’d document the difference and monitor usage of the older version before retiring it. I think the key is not to silently change what an existing client expects from checkout.

**Interviewer (Q14)** [16:13]: Moving on to payment recovery: Payment authorization succeeds, but the order service times out before recording the result. How would your design recover without charging twice?

**Candidate** [17:00]: I’d make the payment request idempotent, so retrying with the same payment reference doesn’t create another charge. Checkout should keep the attempt in a pending state and query the payment provider or consume its later status update to learn the outcome. Once confirmed, it can record that result and continue the order flow. I’d avoid treating a timeout as a payment failure, because the provider may have completed the authorization even though our service didn’t receive the response.

**Interviewer (Q15)** [17:00]: Moving on to caching: What would you cache in a checkout system, and how would you keep stale prices or inventory from causing incorrect orders?

**Candidate** [17:47]: I’d cache data that helps with quick reads, such as product descriptions, but I wouldn’t treat cached price or inventory as authoritative when placing an order. At checkout, I’d confirm those values with the owning services and reserve inventory before accepting the order. If a cached value is out of date, the customer should see the updated price or availability and confirm again where needed. I think that keeps caching useful without letting it decide the final purchase.

**Interviewer (Q16)** [17:47]: Moving on to incident response: During an on-call incident, how would you communicate a checkout outage and your next steps while the root cause is still uncertain?

**Candidate** [18:32]: I’d share what we know, what customers are experiencing, and which checkout paths appear affected, while being clear that the cause is still under investigation. I’d name someone to coordinate updates, keep product and support informed, and give a time for the next update rather than guessing at a fix time. In parallel, I’d check recent changes and service health, and consider a safe rollback or mitigation if the impact is continuing.

**Interviewer (Q17)** [18:32]: Moving on to event publishing: How would you publish an order-created event to Kafka reliably when the order is committed in Postgres?

**Candidate** [19:17]: I’d want to avoid a gap where the order is committed but the event is lost, or the event is published before the order exists. I’d write the order and a pending event record together in the same database transaction, then have a worker publish pending records to Kafka and mark them sent. Since publishing can be retried, consumers should tolerate duplicate events. I’d monitor records that remain pending or fail to publish.

**Interviewer (Q18)** [19:17]: Moving on to event consumption: A Kafka consumer processes the same order event twice; how would you make downstream fulfillment safe?

**Candidate** [20:00]: I’d make the consumer idempotent, so processing the same event again doesn’t create another fulfillment action. It can record the event identifier alongside the work it performs and check that record before applying the change. I’d also make the fulfillment operation itself safe to retry, since failures can happen between completing the action and recording that it was completed. That way, duplicate delivery doesn’t become a duplicate shipment.

**Interviewer (Q19)** [20:00]: Moving on to traffic protection: How would you protect checkout APIs from traffic spikes and abusive clients without blocking legitimate shoppers?

**Candidate** [20:47]: I’d use sensible per-client rate limits and protect the service with a queue or backpressure when demand rises, while keeping limits fair for normal shoppers. I’d watch traffic patterns and error rates so we can distinguish a broad legitimate spike from abusive behavior. For customers who hit a limit, I’d return a clear retry response rather than letting requests pile up indefinitely. I’d also make sure payment and inventory dependencies aren’t overwhelmed by the same burst.

**Interviewer (Q20)** [20:47]: Moving on to database performance: A Postgres query is fast in isolation but slow during peak checkout traffic; what would you investigate?

**Candidate** [21:35]: I’d look at what changes under peak load, such as connection pool wait time, database CPU, locks, and competing queries. A query can be fast by itself but slow when many requests run at once or when connections are exhausted. I’d compare query timings and database metrics during normal and busy periods, then check whether the application is opening too many connections or holding them for too long. I’d avoid changing the query until I understand where the waiting occurs.

**Interviewer (Q21)** [21:35]: Now consider an upcoming event: The business wants a flash sale next week; which parts of your checkout design would you validate first, and what would you defer?

**Candidate** [22:20]: I’d first validate the parts most likely to affect customers during a traffic spike: inventory reservations, payment handling, and the database and service capacity behind checkout. I’d run a realistic load test and confirm that failures or timeouts don’t create duplicate orders or charges. I’d defer broader cleanup or nonessential feature work until after the sale, while making sure there’s a clear monitoring and rollback plan for the changes we do need.

**Interviewer (Q22)** [22:20]: Moving on to reservation timing: How would you design inventory reservations that expire, including what happens if payment completes near the expiration time?

**Candidate** [23:06]: I’d give each reservation an expiration time and have inventory release it if checkout hasn’t completed. For payment finishing near that time, I’d avoid relying only on a local timer; checkout should confirm the reservation is still valid before finalizing the order. If it has expired, the system needs a clear recovery path, such as trying to reserve again or reversing the payment if stock can’t be secured. I’d make those actions safe to retry.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [23:06]: How would you ensure reservation confirmation and expiration cannot both succeed when payment completes near the deadline?

**Candidate** [23:52]: I’d have the inventory service make confirmation and expiration mutually exclusive through an atomic state transition. Both operations would check the current reservation state, and only one could change it from active. If confirmation wins, expiration does nothing; if expiration wins, checkout must treat the reservation as unavailable and follow its recovery path. I’d make the transition safe to retry and have payment handling account for the possibility that inventory confirmation loses the race.

**Interviewer (Q23)** [23:52]: Moving on to design review: A team proposes synchronous calls to every downstream service during checkout. How would you challenge or defend that approach?

**Candidate** [24:39]: I’d ask which downstream results are truly needed before checkout can give the customer a reliable answer. Some synchronous calls may be necessary for current pricing, inventory reservation, and payment, but calling every service adds latency and makes checkout depend on each one being available. I’d keep nonessential work asynchronous where possible, and define timeouts and recovery behavior for the calls that remain. I think the right boundary depends on what the business promises at purchase time.

**Interviewer (Q24)** [24:39]: Moving on to observability: How would you trace one checkout attempt across API calls, database writes, and Kafka events while protecting sensitive customer data?

**Candidate** [25:26]: I’d assign a correlation identifier to the checkout attempt and pass it through service calls and event metadata, so logs and traces can be connected. I’d record useful timings and outcomes around database and Kafka work, but avoid putting card details or other sensitive customer data in those records. Access to telemetry should be controlled, and identifiers should not expose personal information. That would make it easier to follow a failure without logging more than we need.

**Interviewer (Q25)** [25:26]: Moving on to Postgres transactions: What isolation level would you choose for inventory updates and order creation, and how would you handle conflicts?

**Candidate** [26:12]: I’d keep the database transaction short and use an atomic update or reservation check so inventory can’t be oversold. For order creation, I’d use the normal transaction behavior unless the business rules require stronger guarantees across related rows. If concurrent requests conflict, I’d retry safely or return an availability error, using an idempotency key to avoid creating a duplicate order. I’d want to test the actual access pattern before choosing a more restrictive isolation level.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [26:12]: Which Postgres isolation level would you choose for each operation, and how would you detect and handle conflicts?

**Candidate** [26:57]: For ordinary order creation, I’d start with Read Committed and rely on constraints and short transactions for the checks that need to be atomic. For inventory reservation, I’d use an atomic conditional update so only a request with available stock can succeed. I’d detect a conflict when that update affects no row, or through a serialization or deadlock error if one occurs, then retry only when safe or return out of stock.

**Interviewer (Q26)** [26:57]: Moving on to deployment: How would you roll out a new checkout API implementation with minimal risk and roll it back if order failures increase?

**Candidate** [27:44]: I’d roll it out gradually, starting with a small share of traffic while comparing errors and checkout outcomes against the existing implementation. I’d make sure the new version can coexist with the current API and that database changes are backward-compatible. If order failures rise, I’d stop increasing traffic and route requests back to the old implementation. I’d also check for orders left in an uncertain state and reconcile them, rather than assuming rollback alone fixes everything.

**Interviewer (Q27)** [27:44]: Moving on to network failures: A regional network partition separates checkout from the payment provider. What behavior would you choose for in-flight and new orders?

**Candidate** [28:31]: For new orders, I’d avoid claiming payment succeeded when checkout can’t reach the provider. I’d return a clear pending or temporarily unavailable status, depending on whether the request may already have reached payment, and let the customer safely retry with the same idempotency key. For in-flight orders, I’d keep their state uncertain until we can reconcile with the provider, rather than charging again or treating a timeout as a definite failure. I’d communicate the disruption clearly.

**Interviewer (Q28)** [28:31]: Now consider a production issue: After a deployment, duplicate orders increase, but logs show only one API request per order. How would you investigate across the database and event pipeline?

**Candidate** [29:16]: I’d first trace a few duplicate orders using their customer, cart, and correlation identifiers, then check whether the database contains separate order records or one order that was processed twice downstream. I’d inspect idempotency-key handling and transaction boundaries, including whether concurrent requests can bypass the uniqueness check. Then I’d follow the related Kafka events and consumer records to see whether duplication began during publishing or fulfillment, and compare the timing with the deployment.

**Interviewer (Q29)** [29:16]: Moving on to migration: How would you migrate checkout data and traffic from a monolith to independently owned services without losing orders or creating conflicting sources of truth?

**Candidate** [30:02]: I’d first define which service becomes authoritative for each piece of data, so the monolith and new services don’t both make independent decisions. I’d migrate gradually, keeping a way to compare results and reconcile differences before sending more checkout traffic to the new path. I’d be cautious about dual writes, since one side can succeed while the other fails. The rollout should include clear monitoring and a way to pause or reverse traffic safely.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [30:02]: How would you move existing orders and ongoing updates to the new service, then cut over authority without missing or duplicating writes?

**Candidate** [30:50]: I’d copy existing orders in batches, then keep the new service current with changes from the monolith while validating that both sides agree. For the cutover, I’d choose a clear point where new writes go to the new service, and make sure updates arriving during the transition can be recognized and applied only once. I’d avoid having both systems independently accept writes. I’d also keep the old data available for comparison and recovery until the new path is stable.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [30:50]: How would you establish the cutover point and ensure changes between the initial copy and cutover are captured and applied exactly once?

**Candidate** [31:38]: I’d establish a consistent starting point for the copy, then capture every change after that point from the old system’s change log or an outbox. The new service would apply those changes using a stable event or record identifier, so replays are safe. Before switching writes, I’d verify the new service has caught up and agree on a clear authority boundary. I wouldn’t rely on timing alone; I’d reconcile records around the cutover and monitor for lag or duplicates.

**Interviewer (Q30)** [31:38]: For the final question, present your end-to-end checkout design to a skeptical review panel, including its hardest failure mode, operational runbooks, and the trade-off you would revisit first as scale grows.

**Candidate** [32:26]: I’d design checkout to validate pricing, reserve inventory, authorize payment, and then create the order, with each step recorded so we can recover after a timeout. The hardest failure is an uncertain payment outcome, so we’d reconcile with the provider before retrying and keep requests idempotent. The runbook would cover pausing risky traffic, checking dependencies, reconciling pending orders, and communicating customer impact. As scale grows, I’d revisit how much checkout depends on synchronous calls, while preserving clear customer outcomes.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [32:26]: Suppose the new retailer expects 40,000 orders per day. Choose assumptions for the busiest hour, checkout requests per order, retries, and headroom, then calculate the peak requests per second you’d provision for.

**Candidate** [33:10]: I’d assume the busiest hour contains about a tenth of daily orders, with several checkout requests per order, some retry traffic, and extra headroom for bursts. I’d calculate orders in that hour, divide by seconds, multiply by requests per order and retry allowance, then add headroom. I don’t have a reliable figure without choosing precise assumptions, but I’d document those assumptions and validate the estimate with a load test before provisioning.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [33:10]: Could you choose specific values for requests per order, retries, and headroom, then calculate the resulting requests per second?

**Candidate** [33:55]: Assuming the busiest hour contains about a tenth of daily orders, and each order generates four requests, with a ten percent retry allowance and fifty percent headroom, I’d provision for roughly seven requests per second. I’d treat that as an illustrative starting point, since real traffic may be burstier and the checkout flow may use more calls. I’d validate it against retailer traffic patterns and a load test before relying on it.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [33:55]: How would you turn that seven-requests-per-second estimate into a capacity plan for the checkout service, Postgres, and the payment provider?

**Candidate** [34:42]: I’d use the estimate as a starting point for a load test, then measure how the checkout service, Postgres, and payment provider behave as traffic rises. I’d check CPU, connection use, query latency, and provider limits, and identify which component becomes constrained first. I’d provision enough capacity with room for bursts, while setting limits so checkout doesn’t overwhelm dependencies. I’d also confirm the provider’s permitted rate and have a graceful response when that limit is reached.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [34:42]: At the estimated seven requests per second, how would you determine the number of checkout instances and database connections needed? Walk through a numerical example using assumptions of your choice.

**Candidate** [35:31]: For an example, I’d assume each checkout instance can handle a few requests per second while staying within its latency target, then add an instance or two for resilience and test that under load. For Postgres, I’d set a modest connection pool per instance and ensure the total stays within the database’s safe connection limit. I wouldn’t infer those values from seven requests per second alone; I’d measure request time and database use in a representative load test, then adjust the pools.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [35:31]: For that example, suppose an instance handles four requests per second at the latency target and can use up to 10 database connections. How many instances and connections would you configure to handle seven requests per second if one instance fails?

**Candidate** [36:13]: I’d configure three instances, so after one fails the remaining two can handle up to eight requests per second, which is above the expected load. At ten database connections per instance, that gives a maximum of thirty connections across the fleet. I’d still check that Postgres can safely support that total, and set the application’s pool limits accordingly rather than letting every instance open connections without coordination.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [36:13]: How would you define a numerical availability target for checkout and calculate the allowed downtime over a 30-day month?

**Candidate** [36:56]: I’d propose a 99.9% monthly availability target for checkout, measured as successful eligible requests divided by total eligible requests. Over a 30-day month, that allows about 43 minutes of downtime, since 0.1% of the month is roughly that amount. I’d also track customer-visible failures and latency, because an endpoint can technically respond while checkout is still unusable. We’d review the target with product and operations based on customer impact.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [36:56]: What numerical p95 latency target would you propose for checkout, and how would you define which requests count toward it?

**Candidate** [37:38]: I’d propose a p95 target of 500 milliseconds for the checkout API, measured monthly across valid customer checkout requests, including successful responses and transient server errors. I’d exclude expected business rejections, like an unavailable item, but track those separately. I’d measure from the API receiving the request to returning its response, and also monitor end-to-end completion time so a quick pending response doesn’t hide a slow checkout.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [37:38]: How would you measure end-to-end checkout completion time, and what numerical target would you propose for it?

**Candidate** [38:25]: I’d measure from the customer’s initial checkout request until the order reaches a confirmed or clearly failed state, using a correlation ID to connect the steps. I’d propose a p95 target of 10 seconds, while treating cases waiting on an external payment response separately and showing them as pending rather than complete. I’d track the distribution over a monthly window and alert on shorter periods too, so a brief outage isn’t hidden by the monthly average.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [38:25]: How would you include checkouts that remain pending beyond 10 seconds in your end-to-end measurement, and what target would you set for them?

**Candidate** [39:10]: I’d keep pending checkouts in the measurement rather than excluding them, and record how long they take to reach a final outcome. I’d set a separate target that most pending cases are resolved within a few minutes, with alerts for cases that remain unresolved beyond that. The customer response should clearly say the order is still being checked, and operations should reconcile the payment and order state before asking the customer to retry.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [39:10]: What numerical target would you set for the percentage of pending checkouts resolved within a specific time window?

**Candidate** [39:51]: I’d set a target that 99% of pending checkouts reach a confirmed or failed state within five minutes. I’d measure from the initial checkout request until that final state, including cases still pending at the end of the window as misses. I’d review the target against payment-provider behavior and customer expectations, and alert on a shorter time window when the unresolved share starts rising.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [39:51]: Suppose a pending checkout has already held an inventory reservation for five minutes. How would you decide whether to extend the reservation or release it while the payment outcome remains uncertain?

**Candidate** [40:37]: I’d avoid extending the reservation indefinitely, but I also wouldn’t release it while payment may have succeeded without checking the provider. I’d first reconcile the payment outcome using the original idempotency reference. If payment is confirmed, I’d try to secure the inventory and proceed; if it failed, I’d release the reservation. If the provider is still unreachable, I’d follow a bounded policy and keep the order visibly pending, with an operational alert for unresolved cases.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [40:37]: If the provider remains unreachable past the reservation deadline, what specific action would you take, and how would you handle a later confirmation that payment succeeded?

**Candidate** [41:23]: I’d let the reservation expire rather than hold inventory indefinitely, while keeping the order pending and avoiding another payment attempt. I’d continue checking with the provider using the same reference. If payment is later confirmed, I’d try to reserve the inventory again; if it’s no longer available, I’d cancel or refund the payment and clearly update the customer. I’d make those recovery actions safe to retry and ensure the case is visible to support.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [41:23]: If inventory is unavailable after a late payment confirmation, how would you coordinate the refund and order status so retries or worker failures cannot issue multiple refunds?

**Candidate** [42:10]: I’d move the order to a state that clearly records payment succeeded but fulfillment cannot proceed, then initiate a refund using a stable idempotency key tied to that payment. I’d persist the refund request and outcome so a worker retry can reuse the same key rather than start another refund. The order would remain unresolved until the provider confirms the refund, then move to a refunded or canceled state. I’d alert support if confirmation is delayed.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [42:10]: How would you persist the refund request and order state atomically, and recover if the worker crashes after the provider accepts the refund but before recording the result?

**Candidate** [42:57]: I’d update the order state and write a refund task to an outbox in the same database transaction, so the intent isn’t lost. A worker would send the refund with a stable idempotency key and then record the provider result. If it crashes after the provider accepts, the retry would use that same key, or query the provider by reference, before updating the order. That avoids issuing a separate refund just because the local result wasn’t recorded.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [42:57]: How would you detect and resolve a mismatch between the order’s refund status and the payment provider’s records?

**Candidate** [43:46]: I’d run reconciliation using the provider’s refund records and our stored payment and refund references, and flag cases where the statuses disagree. For each mismatch, I’d query the provider for the latest authoritative result before changing the order. If the provider shows a completed refund, I’d update our record; if it’s still pending or failed, I’d keep the order in a clear unresolved state and retry or escalate according to the provider’s response. I’d retain an audit trail of the correction.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [43:46]: Returning to the migration, how would you handle an order update that arrives in the monolith while you switch write authority to the new service?

**Candidate** [44:32]: I’d define a clear cutover point and make the monolith forward updates after that point to the new service, rather than letting both systems independently update the order. I’d give each update a stable identifier or version so the new service can detect replays and reject stale changes. During the transition, I’d monitor the change stream and reconcile orders that are delayed or missing, with a pause option if the new service falls behind.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [44:32]: How would you prevent an update already in flight to the monolith from being missed or overwriting a newer update during cutover?

**Candidate** [45:22]: I’d make the cutover use a durable change stream with a known checkpoint, so updates accepted by the monolith before the switch can still be replayed to the new service. I’d include an order version on each change and have the new service apply it only if it’s newer than the version already stored. Before routing all writes to the new service, I’d wait until the stream is caught up and verify the affected orders, rather than relying only on a deployment timestamp.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [45:22]: How would you establish a version sequence shared by both systems so a delayed monolith update cannot overwrite a newer update from the new service?

**Candidate** [46:10]: I’d use a version assigned by the system that owns the order, and require every update to carry that version. During cutover, I’d transfer the current version with each order and make the new service accept only updates that advance it. If the monolith can still write, both sides need a coordinated way to assign versions, such as a shared sequence or a single writer. I’d avoid relying on timestamps, since clocks and delayed delivery can make ordering unclear.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [46:10]: How would you use a single-writer cutover to handle in-flight monolith updates before the new service begins accepting writes?

**Candidate** [46:57]: I’d briefly stop or queue new order updates at the monolith, then wait for its in-flight writes to finish and drain the durable change stream into the new service. I’d verify the new service has caught up, switch the write route, and only then allow it to accept updates. Any queued requests would be forwarded to the new owner with their identifiers preserved. If validation fails, I’d keep the monolith as the writer and investigate before proceeding.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [46:57]: How would you verify that the change stream has fully drained before switching write authority, and what would you do if the cutover process crashes midway?

**Candidate** [47:43]: I’d compare the monolith’s last committed change position with the new service’s applied position, and confirm there are no unprocessed updates or errors. I’d also reconcile order counts and versions for the affected set before switching authority. I’d record the cutover phase durably, so if the process crashes, we can resume from the last verified checkpoint rather than guessing. Until the new service is confirmed ready, I’d keep writes paused or routed to the monolith.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [47:43]: If the cutover crashes after routing writes to the new service but before recording that the switch completed, how would you recover without allowing both systems to accept writes?

**Candidate** [48:30]: I’d treat the durable routing configuration as the source of truth, not the cutover process’s in-memory state. On recovery, I’d inspect that configuration and verify which service accepted writes, then keep the other service fenced from writes. If the new service was already receiving traffic, I’d resume from that state and reconcile any uncertain requests using their identifiers. I’d avoid automatically switching back until we know the write history is consistent and the authority is clear.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [48:30]: How would you implement write fencing so the monolith cannot accept updates after authority moves to the new service, even if an old process or cached configuration is still active?

**Candidate** [49:15]: I’d require every write to carry an authority token or lease that the database or a shared control point validates, rather than trusting a process’s cached setting. At cutover, I’d advance the authority generation and give write permission only to the new service. The monolith’s old token would then be rejected, even if it is still running. I’d make the fencing check part of the write path and alert on rejected stale writes.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [49:15]: If the monolith and new service use separate databases, where would you enforce that fencing check so a stale monolith process cannot commit an update during cutover?

**Candidate** [50:02]: I’d enforce the write gate at a shared control point that both services must consult, rather than relying on either database to know about the other. For example, each write could require a current authority lease or generation, checked by a strongly consistent coordinator before it is accepted. The monolith should fail closed if it cannot verify its authority. I’d also revoke its credentials or network access after cutover as an additional safeguard, not the sole control.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [50:02]: If a monolith write validates its lease, then the lease is revoked before that write commits, how would you prevent the stale write from committing?

**Candidate** [50:49]: I’d make lease validation and the write commit part of a coordinated operation, rather than checking once and assuming authority remains valid. One approach is to use a fencing generation that the storage layer checks at commit time, rejecting writes with an older generation. If the systems use separate databases and can’t share that check, I’d pause and drain monolith writes before revoking authority, then verify no transactions remain in flight before enabling the new writer.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [50:49]: How would you fence the monolith and verify that all its in-flight database transactions have finished before enabling writes in the new service?

**Candidate** [51:36]: I’d first block new monolith writes at a shared gateway or database permission boundary, then wait for its active write transactions to finish or explicitly cancel them. I’d verify there are no remaining write sessions and that the monolith’s change stream is fully applied in the new service. Only after those checks would I enable new-service writes. If a transaction is still uncertain, I’d keep the cutover paused and reconcile its order rather than risk two writers.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [51:36]: How would you distinguish active monolith write transactions from other database sessions, and prevent a new write transaction from starting while you inspect them?

**Candidate** [52:23]: I’d identify the monolith’s write sessions using a dedicated database role or application name, so they can be separated from read-only or unrelated traffic. To prevent new writes during inspection, I’d first close the write gate and revoke or restrict that role’s write permissions, then check for remaining active transactions and let them finish or cancel them. I’d verify the gate is effective before relying on the session list, since a snapshot alone can become stale.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [52:23]: How would you test that the write gate also blocks a monolith transaction that began before permissions were revoked?

**Candidate** [53:08]: I’d test this in a staging environment with a transaction that starts a write, pauses before commit, and then revoke the monolith’s write permission. I’d check whether that existing transaction can still commit, since permission changes may not cancel work already in progress. If it can, the cutover procedure must explicitly drain or terminate those transactions before enabling the new writer. I’d also verify that new writes are rejected after the gate closes.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [53:08]: How would you verify, using Postgres, that no transaction capable of committing a monolith write remains before enabling the new writer?

**Candidate** [53:54]: I’d first block new monolith writes, then inspect Postgres activity and transaction state for sessions using the monolith’s dedicated role or application name. I’d also check for prepared transactions, since they can remain unresolved after a client disconnects. I’d wait for active write transactions to finish or terminate them, then verify the session list and transaction state are clear before enabling the new writer. I’d keep the gate closed if any session’s status is uncertain.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [53:54]: How would you handle an unresolved prepared transaction before enabling the new writer, and verify its outcome has reached the new service?

**Candidate** [54:41]: I’d keep the new writer disabled until the prepared transaction is resolved, rather than assuming it will disappear. I’d identify its transaction and order, determine whether it committed or should be rolled back, and resolve it through the appropriate recovery process. Then I’d confirm the resulting order update appears in the monolith’s durable change stream and has been applied by the new service, checking its version or state before opening writes. I’d record the decision for later audit.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [54:41]: How would you decide whether to commit or roll back an unresolved prepared transaction without knowing the original coordinator’s decision?

**Candidate** [55:28]: I wouldn’t guess based only on the prepared transaction record. I’d look for durable evidence from the coordinator or transaction log showing the intended decision, and correlate it with the order and any downstream effects. If the decision can’t be established, I’d keep writes fenced and escalate to the database and service owners for a controlled recovery decision. After resolving it, I’d reconcile the order and payment state before allowing the new service to accept writes.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [55:28]: How would you design the order-creation API’s request and response contracts, including idempotency and validation errors?

**Candidate** [56:14]: I’d have the request include a cart reference and an idempotency key, with customer identity coming from the authenticated session. The response would include an order identifier and a status such as pending or confirmed. For validation errors, I’d return a clear, stable error code and a message that helps the client correct the cart, without creating an order. A retry with the same key should return the original result rather than create a duplicate.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [56:14]: How would the API handle concurrent requests using the same idempotency key, including a retry whose request body differs from the original?

**Candidate** [57:01]: I’d store the idempotency key with the authenticated customer and a fingerprint of the original request, protected by a uniqueness constraint. If two matching requests arrive together, only one should create the order; the other waits or returns the stored result. If the same key arrives with a different request body, I’d reject it as a conflict rather than silently reuse a result for different cart contents. I’d make sure the stored response is available for later retries.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [57:01]: If the first request crashes after claiming the idempotency key but before storing a response, how would a retry recover safely?

**Candidate** [57:49]: I’d keep the idempotency record and order creation in the same database transaction where possible, so a crash either commits both or neither. If some work happens outside that transaction, I’d store a durable in-progress state with enough information to resume or reconcile it. A retry would check that state and continue the original attempt rather than create another order. I’d use a lease or timeout only with care, so a slow original request isn’t mistaken for a failed one.

**Interviewer** [57:49]: Thank you for your time. Your interview report is being prepared.

</details>

## Run 16: 30 min Medium Project management, Technical Project Manager (average candidate)

**Overall 42/100.** Communication 5, technical 3, confidence 3, problem-solving 5, relevance 5. AI cost ₹14.00.

**What went well:** Communicate with restraint; Prioritize release safety; Respect decision ownership

**To improve:** Substantiate your examples; Make plans actionable; Add payments detail; Be more decisive

**Answers to work on:** Explain rollout mechanics (What specific rollout approach allowed the team to migrate without downtime, and what checks governed continuation or rollback?); Propose measurable triggers (What specific metrics and thresholds would you propose for pausing a payments rollout, and how would you choose them?); Show risk management (Can you give a specific example of a migration risk you tracked, including how it was monitored and mitigated?); Quantify estimation uncertainty (How would you work with engineering leads to estimate a new payments feature when the requirements are still evolving?); Define commitment criteria (How would you decide when there is enough information to commit to a release date?); Set an incident cadence (What would you communicate to stakeholders during the first 15 minutes of that incident, and how frequently would you update them?); Specify refund evidence (Suppose the incomplete check covers refunds through the new provider. What evidence would you need before deciding whether the release can proceed?); Distinguish action from decision (Can you describe a specific decision you personally made during the payments migration and how you made it?)

**Candidate** [27:25]: I’d first clarify what the check is intended to prove, why it remains incomplete, and what risk that leaves. If we can’t establish that the release is safe, I’d recommend delaying and escalate the decision to the accountable leads.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [27:25]: Suppose the incomplete check covers refunds through the new provider. What evidence would you need before deciding whether the release can proceed?

**Candidate** [28:07]: I’d ask the payments and engineering owners to clarify the expected refund behavior, the likely customer impact, and why the check is incomplete. I’d look for reliable test evidence or another accepted way to confirm the behavior, and make sure any remaining risk is understood by the accountable business and technical leads. If that evidence isn’t available, I’d recommend delaying rather than assume refunds will work correctly.

## Run 17: 30 min Medium Project management, Technical Project Manager (average candidate)

**Overall 46/100.** Communication 5, technical 4, confidence 4, problem-solving 5, relevance 5. AI cost ₹13.62.

**What went well:** Clear stakeholder communication; Sound safety instincts; Responsible commitment management

**To improve:** Make examples concrete; Deepen payments knowledge; Structure planning decisions; State decisions directly

**Answers to work on:** Name cutover dependencies (Which specific integrations and technical dependencies would you verify before setting the cutover date?); Explain cross-provider controls (What specific mechanism would prevent the new provider from processing that retry when the original provider has already charged the customer?); Define rollback evidence (What release and rollback checks would you put in place to support a no-downtime payments migration?); Specify reconciliation records (What specific transaction records would you compare across the two providers during reconciliation?); Show estimation method (How do you work with engineering leads to estimate a cross-team release?); Use an actual risk (Can you give an example of a payments-specific risk you identified early and how you tracked it?); Substantiate migration impact (What specific decisions or actions did you own during the migration, and how was the reduction in transaction costs measured?); Recall a migration lesson (What specific issue arose during the five-month migration, and how would you use that experience to plan differently next time?)

**Candidate** [13:08]: I’d bring both teams together to clarify what the dependency is, what work remains, and how it affects the release. I’d look at the existing ownership agreements and ask the leads to agree on a clear owner and next steps. I think my role is to keep the discussion focused on resolving the blocker, rather than on blame, and make sure stakeholders understand any schedule impact.

**Interviewer (Q14)** [13:08]: Let’s consider a stakeholder request. A senior stakeholder asks you to commit to a date before engineering estimates are ready. What would you say?

**Candidate** [13:47]: I’d explain that I can share a planning target, but I wouldn’t present it as a firm commitment until engineering has reviewed the scope and dependencies. I’d ask when they need an update and agree to return with a better-supported date. I think it’s important to be responsive while being clear about what we know and what still needs confirmation.

**Interviewer (Q15)** [13:47]: Let’s turn to lessons from your migration. How would you apply what you learned from the five-month provider migration to plan a new cross-team payments release?

**Candidate** [14:29]: I’d start by bringing the teams and stakeholders together to clarify the scope, dependencies, and readiness expectations. I’d keep the release plan visible, assign clear owners, and make sure testing and communication are included from the start. I think the migration reinforced that plans need regular review, so I’d raise changes early and adjust the plan with the teams rather than assume everything will go as expected.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [14:29]: What specific issue arose during the five-month migration, and how would you use that experience to plan differently next time?

**Candidate** [15:05]: I don’t have a specific issue from the migration that I can confidently describe. In general, I’d use the experience to make dependencies and ownership clear earlier, and review them regularly with the teams. I think that helps surface problems before they affect the release plan, while keeping stakeholders informed about changes.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [15:05]: For a new payments provider cutover, how would you check that in-flight transactions can be reconciled if the team needs to roll back?

**Candidate** [15:48]: I’d ask the engineering and finance teams to confirm how transaction records are tracked across both providers and how any in-flight payments would be reviewed after a rollback. I’d want the team to agree on a clear reconciliation process and verify it during testing, including who owns investigating any mismatches. I think we should not proceed until the responsible teams are comfortable that payments can be accounted for.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [15:48]: What specific transaction records would you compare across the two providers during reconciliation?

**Candidate** [16:28]: I’d ask the teams to compare the transaction records needed to confirm that each payment was handled consistently by both providers, including its status and any related outcome. I’d also have them agree on how to investigate records that don’t match. I think the exact fields should be confirmed by the engineering and finance teams based on how the providers report transactions.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [16:28]: How would you test the rollback process before cutover, including what happens to an in-flight payment?

**Candidate** [17:09]: I’d ask the teams to walk through a rollback in a test environment and confirm how an in-flight payment is tracked before and after switching back. They should verify that it isn’t lost or processed twice, and that any uncertain outcomes can be reviewed and reconciled. I’d make sure there’s a clear owner for the checks and that the results are understood before cutover.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [17:09]: How would you decide whether to retry a payment whose outcome is uncertain after a rollback?

**Candidate** [17:46]: I’d avoid retrying it until the team has checked the available records and confirmed whether the original payment succeeded. I’d involve the engineering and payments stakeholders in agreeing on the decision, so we don’t risk duplicate processing. I think the process should be defined and tested before cutover, with a clear owner for resolving uncertain cases.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [17:46]: What would you do if the provider’s records still don’t confirm whether the payment succeeded?

**Candidate** [18:24]: I’d keep the payment in an unresolved state rather than assume it failed and retry it. I’d escalate it to the payments and engineering owners, follow the agreed investigation process, and make sure the customer or support team is handled appropriately. I think the key is to avoid creating a duplicate while the outcome is still uncertain.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [18:24]: What information would you ask the provider for to investigate that unresolved payment?

**Candidate** [19:02]: I’d ask the provider to investigate the payment using the details needed to identify it and confirm its current status. I’d also ask whether it was accepted, completed, or declined, and whether there were any related attempts. I think we should share only the information needed for the investigation and follow our normal security and support process.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [19:02]: What specific identifiers would you use to trace the payment with the provider?

**Candidate** [19:43]: I’d use the payment reference or transaction identifier recorded by our system, along with the relevant provider reference if one was returned. I’d ask the team to confirm which identifiers are available and safe to share through the provider’s support process. I think the important thing is to match the same payment across our records and the provider’s records without exposing unnecessary customer information.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [19:43]: What criteria would you use to decide that a payments cutover should be paused before it begins?

**Candidate** [20:25]: I’d pause the cutover if a key readiness check was still unresolved, the teams lacked confidence in testing, or there wasn’t a clear way to respond to problems. I’d ask the responsible leads to explain the gap and what is needed to close it, then communicate the effect on the plan. I think it’s better to revisit the date than proceed with an important concern still open.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [20:25]: What specific payment-processing signals would you monitor during cutover, and what would trigger a rollback?

**Candidate** [21:07]: I’d ask engineering to monitor whether payments are being accepted and completed as expected, and whether there are signs of failures or unusual delays. If the team saw a sustained issue or couldn’t confirm payment outcomes, I’d pause and follow the agreed rollback process. I think the exact signals and thresholds should be set with the technical owners before cutover, so everyone knows when to act.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [21:07]: How would you work with engineering to set a rollback threshold for an increase in payment failures?

**Candidate** [21:49]: I’d ask engineering to review the normal failure patterns and agree on what level of change would indicate a real problem. We’d define how long to observe it, who monitors it, and who can call for rollback. I think the threshold should be written into the release plan and checked before cutover, so the team can respond consistently rather than debate it during an incident.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [21:49]: Suppose the normal payment failure rate is 1%, and it rises to 5% after cutover. What would you do next?

**Candidate** [22:29]: I’d treat that as a serious signal and quickly ask engineering to verify the data and whether the issue is affecting customers. I’d follow the agreed rollback or pause criteria rather than wait for the problem to grow, while keeping stakeholders informed. I think the team should also preserve the relevant information so it can investigate the cause after payments are stable.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [22:29]: If the agreed rollback threshold is a 3% failure rate sustained for five minutes, and the rate has been 5% for six minutes, what would you do?

**Candidate** [23:06]: I’d follow the agreed rollback process, since the observed failure rate has crossed the threshold for longer than the specified period. I’d have the release owner and engineering lead confirm the trigger, coordinate the rollback, and keep stakeholders informed. I’d also make sure the team monitors payment processing afterward and preserves details for the investigation.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [23:06]: During a rollback, how would you coordinate engineering and finance to identify and resolve payments left in an uncertain state?

**Candidate** [23:46]: I’d bring the engineering and finance owners together and make sure they share a single list of unresolved payments. Engineering can help confirm the technical status, while finance can support the reconciliation process. I’d assign clear ownership for each follow-up, keep uncertain payments from being retried without review, and provide stakeholders with a brief update on progress and any customer impact.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [23:46]: For a payment that appears as successful in the provider’s records but pending in your system, how would you coordinate its resolution?

**Candidate** [24:27]: I’d ask engineering to trace the payment through our system and confirm why the status hasn’t updated, while finance checks the provider record and reconciliation process. I’d keep it visible as unresolved until the teams agree on the correct status, and avoid triggering another payment attempt in the meantime. I think we should follow the established process and communicate any customer impact clearly.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [24:27]: What evidence would you require before updating your system’s pending payment to successful?

**Candidate** [25:06]: I’d ask the responsible teams to confirm the provider’s success record matches our payment reference and that there isn’t a conflicting result in our system. I’d also want the normal reconciliation checks completed and the update recorded through the agreed process. I think the engineering and finance owners should confirm the evidence is sufficient before changing the payment status.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [25:06]: What checks would you put in place to prevent a payment from being processed twice during a provider cutover?

**Candidate** [25:47]: I’d ask engineering to make sure each payment can be matched to a unique reference and that retries are checked against the existing payment status. I’d also want the team to test what happens when the outcome is unclear, so a second attempt isn’t made until the first one is investigated. I think the controls and ownership should be clear before the cutover begins.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [25:47]: How would you test that a retry sent to the new provider does not create a duplicate payment if the original provider’s response was lost?

**Candidate** [26:30]: I’d ask engineering to run a test where the original provider processes the payment but its response isn’t received, then check what happens when the retry is sent to the new provider. The team should confirm that the payment can be identified and that the retry is safely handled rather than charged again. I’d have the results reviewed before cutover and make sure any unresolved behavior is addressed.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [26:30]: What specific mechanism would prevent the new provider from processing that retry when the original provider has already charged the customer?

**Candidate** [27:12]: I’d ask engineering to confirm the mechanism they use to recognize a retry as the same payment and prevent it from being processed again. I think the key is that this control works across the provider change, not only within one provider. I’d want the team to demonstrate the behavior in testing and explain how an uncertain original outcome is checked before a retry is allowed.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [27:12]: Suppose neither provider can deduplicate across providers. How would you plan the cutover to avoid retrying a payment whose original outcome is unknown?

**Candidate** [27:52]: I’d avoid automatically retrying payments with an unknown outcome during the cutover. I’d ask the teams to hold those cases for review, check provider records, and reconcile them before deciding whether another attempt is safe. I think we’d also need a clear customer and support process for delays, and engineering should confirm how the cutover handles those payments before we proceed.

**Interviewer** [27:52]: Thank you for your time and for sharing your experience. Your report is being prepared.

**Interviewer (Q11)** [6:51]: Let’s move on. How did you assess and mitigate the risk of downtime during the provider migration?

**Candidate** [7:19]: We reviewed rollout readiness with engineering and operations, identified potential failure points, and agreed on monitoring and escalation. I’m not sure of the exact technical safeguards, but continuity and a clear response plan were key.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [7:19]: Can you describe a specific downtime risk you identified and what the team did to mitigate it?

**Candidate** [7:46]: A key risk was disruption if traffic shifted before the new provider path was ready. The team staged the transition, checked readiness, and monitored the change closely, with escalation paths available if issues appeared.

**Interviewer (Q12)** [7:46]: Let’s move on. A stakeholder asks to add a feature to an upcoming release. How would you assess and communicate its impact?

**Candidate** [8:13]: I’d clarify the value and urgency, then ask engineering to assess effort, dependencies, and risk. I’d compare that with current commitments and communicate the trade-offs, including whether scope or timing needs to change.

**Interviewer (Q13)** [8:13]: Let’s move on. Tell me about a time an estimate changed during delivery. How did you respond?

**Candidate** [8:42]: When an estimate changes, I’d first understand what new information affected it, then review the impact with the team. I’d update stakeholders on options and revise scope or timing based on priorities. I don’t recall a specific case.

**Interviewer (Q14)** [8:42]: Let’s move on. How would you use Scrum practices to manage a cross-team payments release without losing sight of the release date?

**Candidate** [9:09]: I’d use sprint planning and reviews to track progress, but keep a separate release view for cross-team dependencies and readiness. I’d review risks regularly and adjust scope early if the date is threatened.

**Interviewer (Q15)** [9:09]: Let’s move on. A team in Berlin says a dependency from Bengaluru will arrive late. How would you lead the teams through the issue?

**Candidate** [9:35]: I’d clarify the dependency, impact, and latest useful delivery date, then bring both leads together to agree on ownership and options. I’d communicate any release impact and track the follow-up visibly.

**Interviewer (Q16)** [9:35]: Let’s move on. How would you handle a disagreement with an engineering lead who believes a deadline is unrealistic?

**Candidate** [10:02]: I’d ask them to explain the assumptions and risks, then review options with the team, such as reducing scope or changing timing. I’d communicate the evidence and make sure the decision is understood.

**Interviewer (Q17)** [10:02]: Let’s move on. During the provider migration, how did you balance the goal of reducing transaction costs with delivery and operational risks?

**Candidate** [10:28]: I treated cost improvement as important, but not at the expense of payment reliability. We reviewed readiness and risk before proceeding, and kept stakeholders informed about the trade-offs and delivery status.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [10:28]: Can you describe a specific trade-off you made between transaction cost savings and operational risk during the migration?

**Candidate** [10:54]: We avoided rushing the cutover just to realize savings sooner. The trade-off was accepting a slower path to cost benefits in favor of stronger readiness checks and protecting service continuity.

**Interviewer (Q18)** [10:54]: Let’s move on. How would you communicate a likely release delay to business stakeholders who have already announced the launch?

**Candidate** [11:22]: I’d communicate as soon as the risk is credible, explain the cause and impact plainly, and offer options with realistic dates. I’d coordinate on a consistent message and keep updates regular as the plan changes.

## Run 20: 60 min Easy Behavioral interview, Customer Success Manager (average candidate)

**Overall 46/100.** Communication 5, technical 4, confidence 5, problem-solving 4, relevance 5. AI cost ₹39.47.

**What went well:** Clear customer communication; Customer-centered judgment; Honest about uncertainty

**To improve:** Make examples concrete; Show technical understanding; Quantify business outcomes

**Answers to work on:** Show the playbook impact (Tell me about your role in rebuilding the onboarding playbook.); Explain the escalation resolution (Describe a time you handled an escalation for one of your top accounts.); Connect action to renewal (Tell me about a time you changed your approach to keep a customer on track toward renewal.); Specify the usage evidence (Tell me about a time you used product usage data to support a customer conversation.); Make the difficult message concrete (Tell me about a time you had to deliver a message a customer might not have wanted to hear.); Clarify the feedback outcome (Describe a time you shared customer feedback with a product team.)

<details><summary>Transcript</summary>

**Interviewer (Q1)** [0:00]: Welcome, and thanks for joining this mock interview. We’ll explore your customer success experience through a series of friendly, practical questions. Tell me about your current role and the customers you support.

**Candidate** [0:36]: I’m currently a customer success manager, supporting mid-market customers. I work with them on onboarding, adoption, and resolving issues, while also helping them get ongoing value from the product. I think a big part of my role is building trust and keeping communication clear, especially when a customer is facing a challenge.

**Interviewer (Q2)** [0:36]: Thank you. Tell me about a customer relationship you enjoyed building.

**Candidate** [1:21]: I enjoyed building a relationship with a customer who needed extra support during onboarding. I made time to understand what they were trying to achieve and kept communication steady as we worked through the early challenges. Over time, the conversations became more open, and we could focus on helping them get value rather than just solving immediate issues. I think that trust is one of the most rewarding parts of customer success.

**Interviewer (follow-up)** [1:21]: What was one specific challenge you helped this customer work through, and what happened as a result?
